Mamadou Coulibaly (AS Monaco) s'excuse après son geste polémique

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Auteur de son premier but dans l'élite lors du match nul sur le terrain sarthois, Mamadou Coulibaly a suscité une vive polémique en mimant un geste d'égorgement. Le jeune milieu de l'AS Monaco se retrouve désormais sous la menace directe d'une lourde suspension.

Dimanche 8 novembre 2026, l'AS Monaco a été accrochée sur la pelouse du Mans FC (1-1) pour le compte de la 10e journée de Ligue 1. Si le club du Rocher espérait engranger des points précieux, la rencontre a été marquée par le geste de son joueur de 22 ans.

Titularisé dans l'entrejeu monégasque, Mamadou Coulibaly pensait passer une bonne soirée après avoir ouvert le score à la 40e minute de jeu. Mais la joie d'inscrire son tout premier but en Ligue 1 a rapidement viré au malaise lors de sa célébration. Le milieu monégasque s'est en effet dirigé vers le poteau de corner pour glisser sur les genoux, avant de tirer la langue et de mimer un geste d'égorgement avec son pouce sous la gorge. Un geste polémique.

Ce geste n'est malheureusement pas inédit en Principauté cette saison. En effet, cette célébration reproduit à l'identique celle effectuée par son partenaire de club Paris Brunner face à Strasbourg en septembre dernier, lors de la 4e journée de Ligue 1. Pour ce geste, le jeune attaquant avait été sanctionné par la commission de discipline de la LFP de quatre matches de suspension, dont deux fermes et deux avec sursis.

D'ailleurs, cette récidive au sein du même effectif met le joueur dans une situation extrêmement délicate. En raison de ce précédent disciplinaire très récent, Mamadou Coulibaly risque une sanction similaire d'au moins deux matches de suspension ferme devant la commission de discipline de la LFP. L'instance disciplinaire devrait se saisir rapidement du dossier pour trancher sur ce comportement. Un coup dur en perspective.

Conscient des remous provoqués par son geste, le joueur de 22 ans s'est exprimé devant les journalistes à l'issue de la rencontre. « Je tiens à m’excuser parce que j’ai cru comprendre que ça avait été mal compris. C’est un geste que j’ai fait par rapport un ami à moi qui m’a conseillé au sujet de la célébration. Je tiens à m'excuser si ça a pu heurter des gens. Maintenant, ce sont des choses qui se font dans d'autres championnats, je n'ai pas pensé que ça pourrait avoir des conséquences négatives », a ainsi déclaré Mamadou Coulibaly en zone mixte au micro des médias après Le Mans-Monaco.

Toutefois, cette justification risque de ne pas suffire auprès de son entraîneur Filipe Luis. Le coach monégasque avait pourtant publiquement averti son groupe en septembre après l'affaire similaire, insistant sur l'exemplarité attendue : « Il a fait une erreur. Il l'a compris tout de suite. Il s'est excusé. Le club prend cette affaire très au sérieux (…). On espère que ça ne se reproduira plus », avait prévenu le technicien en conférence de presse. Au-delà de cette polémique extra-sportive, l'AS Monaco accueillera l'AJ Auxerre au stade Louis-II le samedi 21 novembre 2026 à 20h45 pour la 11e journée de Ligue 1.

Mercato - Ça bouge à l’OM : Qui doit être le prochain joueur à partir cet été ?

Man Utd, Celtic & Palace considering options? The best managers currently out of work

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Former managers of Liverpool, Manchester City and Tottenham are among the biggest names currently available.

With clubs like Manchester United, Celtic and Crystal Palace all rumoured to be considering a change in the dug-out, there are some proven managers and promising coaches who are ready to work.

We’ve ranked the nine best coaches who are currently available.

Does Bielsa have one more job in him at the age of 71?

One of the most respected and influential coaches of his generation, the Argentinian remains a unique figure in football. When it clicks, it clicks spectacularly. Just ask Leeds United fans.

It would be a real shame if such a brilliant, idiosyncratic manager bowed out after Uruguay’s underwhelming World Cup campaign and all the bad blood that followed.

Surely there’s one last club out there willing to take a punt on El Loco?

We’ve forgotten a bit about Motta, given it’s over a year since he was last in work.

He didn’t pull up any trees at Juventus, but there was a reason he landed the job in the first place, having worked wonders at Bologna

And you look at the way that things have gone at Juventus since his sacking, and the problems were surely bigger than him.

We could easily see him back in Serie A, continuing the league’s fine managerial merry-go-round tradition of the same names at the same clubs (Max Allegri at Napoli, you say? Sure) but we’d love to see him test himself in another country.

It only took a matter of weeks for Frank’s successor at Tottenham to rant about the team “lacking in attack, midfield, defence and brain”.

Superb copy, Igor Tudor, we can only thank you.

The Dane didn’t rise to the occasion at Tottenham, that much is clear, but his steady, proven record over multiple seasons at Brentford should keep him in contention for solid, midtable jobs. Crystal Palace look most likely to hire Frank.

“Available” with an admittedly pretty big asterisk.

As with Jurgen Klopp when he left Liverpool, we’re completely inclined to take Howe at his word when he says he needs a rest. You could see the toll that the Newcastle job took on him.

We’d be amazed if Howe is back in work before Christmas, at the earliest, but it’s surely only a matter of time before he starts getting linked to vacancies again.

Our early money’s on Everton when David Moyes leaves. Howe is famously a boyhood Evertonian, and it feels like the right fit. If that goes well, maybe we’ll finally see him managing England down the line.

But Celtic, with whom Howe already held talks, might be on. Watch this space.

Two underwhelming major tournaments at the helm of Germany have seen Nagelsmann’s stock drop a bit. His next job in the club game will be something of a salvage job when it comes to his reputation.

It’ll be interesting to see what kind of job he gets next, with bags of experience at the age of 38. Taking RB Leipzig to the Champions League semi-finals and performing broadly to par with Bayern Munich, delivering an obligatory Bundesliga title, should see him land a decent European-level gig.

Back to the Bundesliga when the Borussia Dortmund job next comes up, maybe? We could certainly see him take on a new challenge in the Premier League, too.

We predicted six clubs where Slot could find himself next after his Liverpool sacking.

The Netherlands job was inevitably mentioned after Ronald Koeman’s departure, but that one ended up going to Xavi.

Deschamps’ time with France is over but he is still only 57, suggesting his managerial career is not at an end.

He has club coaching experience too having led Monaco, Juventus and Marseille, winning trophies with all of them, and so should be a strong contender for any top job if he fancies it.

If a club is ambitious and doesn’t have the distraction of European football, Conte’s track record speaks for itself – an instant impact and titles delivered at Juventus, Chelsea, Inter and Napoli.

His name has always been mentioned with regard to Manchester United’s trevails in the post-Ferguson era.

He probably isn’t the man to build a successful, sustainable long-term ‘project’ but a rocket up the arse might just give them a temporary reprieve in their ongoing psychodrama.

Your reminder that Amazon Prime are currently shooting their All or Nothing: Manchester United documentary series. Conte working under Sir Jim Ratcliffe? Pure box office.

The Catalans’ decade of unprecedented success at the Etihad means he’s earned himself a rest – but could a national team job tempt him out of any sabbatical?

It doesn’t look as though he’ll be throwing himself into another job any time soon, and managing another Premier League club feels borderline impossible, but that won’t stop the tabloids from inevitably putting his name into the hat for every big job that emerges.

We’re fascinated to see what happens next. As long as it isn’t an easy cash grab out in the Middle East.

Rockstar were "definitely" thinking about giving a Japanese studio the keys to GTA, says former tech director - "I have a name, but I'm not gonna say it"

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Obbe Vermeij on ancient plans for a GTA Tokyo

Since time immemorial, North American and European publishers have racked their brains over ways to make their games sell in Japan, ancient heartland of mythical beings like Mario, Sonic, and Polaroid Pete. In the late noughties, Microsoft launched a big push to give Japanese youth the Xbox virus with such targeted console exclusives as Blue Dragon and Lost Odyssey. And Rockstar once dreamed of making a Grand Theft Auto game set in Tokyo.

Speaking to me last week, Rockstar North's former technical director Obbe Vermeij shared a little about the abandoned project. Emphasis on the "little" – it doesn't appear to have gotten very far, despite recently unearthed allusions to "Tokyo PS2" in GTA 5's source code.

Rockstar's parent company Take-Two Interactive filed a trademark for GTA Tokyo back in 2003, the year before the release of the Los Angeles-flavoured GTA: San Andreas. At the time, Rockstar were apparently looking at collaborating with a Japanese studio on the project. "So this was kind above my pay grade," Vermeij told me. "But yeah, there were negotiations with studios, and I have a name, but I'm not gonna say it, because then it becomes like a bigger thing than it is, and I'll probably get it wrong, and I don't know how far the negotiations were, how serious it was. I mean, it was definitely serious. It was the plan. But I don't know how close it got to actually happening."

The game itself would likely have been a contemporary story, not a historical yarn like Rockstar's subsequent Red Dead Redemption. "It definitely had samurai swords in it, I remember that, but I think it was present-day probably," Vermeij went on. "Or sort of not too [far off] - it wasn't, like, sort of the medieval era in Japanese history, or anything. It was definitely not like that."

Vermeij was at Rockstar from 1995 to 2009, where he worked on the first four mainline 3D GTA games, plus some handheld spin-offs. During that time, he says, Rockstar frequently considered the idea of a Grand Theft Auto set outside the United States. "In the same way that we were always thinking about other games, we also thought about cities that would be cool, and it was always the same list," he said. "It was Moscow, London. Istanbul, Rio de Janeiro, maybe Paris and Tokyo. It was always the same ones that people suggested."

Grand Theft Auto has never quite the money-spinner in Japan that it is in North America and Europe. Localised editions of the original three 3D GTA games were published by Capcom, sometime after the original releases, selling a few hundred thousand copies apiece in their launch years.

"I think perhaps [Rockstar's co-founders] the Housers were a little annoyed that GTA wasn't as big in Japan as they thought it deserved to be," Vermeij reflected. "So it kind of made sense to get a local studio to do it. Japanese people actually kind of like different games from westerners a fair bit. For instance, they don't like first person shooters and stuff. So, it made sense to have a Japanese studio have a shot at it, and I think it would have been really cool, because you know, a GTA made by somebody else in Japanese culture – that's something I would probably play."

Take-Two ultimately abandoned their original "GTA Tokyo" trademark in 2008. It's not clear how much Rockstar have considered setting GTA overseas since Vermeij's day, but co-founder Dan Houser (who left the company in 2020) recently told podcaster Lex Fridman that they ultimately decided it wasn't a credible objective.

“We made a little thing in London 26 years ago - GTA London - for the top-down for PS1," he said. "That was pretty cute and fun, as the first mission pack ever for PlayStation 1. I think for a full GTA game, we always decided there was so much Americana inherent in the IP, it would be really hard to make it work in London or anywhere else. You know, you needed guns, you needed these larger-than-life characters. It just felt like the game was so much about America, possibly from an outsider's perspective. But that was so much about what the thing was that it wouldn't really have worked in the same way elsewhere.”

If this article has left you pining for the Shibuya Scramble NPC-tipping sim that will never be, I bring good news: Japanese developers are quite capable of making their own urban open worlders. The obvious go-tos are Sega's Yakuza/Like A Dragon games, with their abundance of lovingly modelled eateries and discount stores. There's also Ghostwire: Tokyo, a respectable first-person parkour sim, though this particular incarnation of Tokyo has a lot of paranormal congestion. All these games have the advantage of being playable on PC right now. As for GTA 6? Who can say, but Vermeij thinks Rockstar might want to port it sooner to get ahead of the slopcomp craze.

5 Yoga Poses to Offset the Effects of Your Daily Commute

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

For many, getting from point A to B is a standard part of life. If this is you, unless you can walk (lucky!), ride a bike (admirable!), or fly (unlikely) to your daily destinations, chances are you spend a great deal of time sitting in a car, bus, or train.

Commuting affects your body in ways that are likely familiar to you. In case your lived experience isn’t evidence enough, research shows that prolonged commuting is associated with musculoskeletal pain including lower back pain and tension through the shoulders and neck.

Fortunately, certain yoga poses—including those that double as lower back stretches or that release your upper body—can help you leave that unwanted tightness in the car, or whatever your preferred form of public transit. Popping into these shapes is simple and relief can come in mere moments, so try moving through your favorite stretch (or flow through them all) as soon as you arrive at your chosen destination.

You’ll notice that a couple of these poses include slight modifications, including generously bent knees, designed to help your body relax, rather than tense even more, as you stretch things out. And if you’re stretching at home, grab a bolster, blocks, or whatever props you need to help you let it all go.

One of the best lower back stretches involves letting it all go. Practicing Standing Forward Bend is a reliable way to elongate your spine and release your tense shoulders. And by bending your knees and letting your arms hang loose—hands either reaching toward your mat or grabbing opposite elbows—you allow gravity to take over, decompressing your spine and inviting neck, back, and shoulder tension to melt away.

Flowing between Cat Pose and Cow Pose is among the most literal (and relaxing) embodiments of your breath available to you. Exhale as you round your back. Inhale as you arch your back. It’s also an effective means of relieving held tension in your back, neck, and shoulders. Cat exaggerates the daily rounding of your spine, the tuck of your chin releasing your neck while your tucked tailbone offers a welcome lower back stretch.

Meanwhile, lifting your chin and dropping your belly (or udders) in Cow takes the pressure off of your shoulders while stretching your neck.

Stepping back to Downward-Facing Dog provides a full-body stretch. As you pedal or sway your way into stillness, keep more of your weight in your legs than your arms, and you’ll likely notice your shoulders opening and relaxing. The moment you let your head get heavy, your stiff neck will thank you.

If your spine needs a little extra love (and length) in Down Dog, try bending your knees and taking the focus off of your hamstrings. This subtle variation invites your spine to fully lengthen, countering some of the natural rounding that can come with sitting for extended periods of times.

There’s a reason Sphinx is a go-to yoga pose for posture correction. Rebranded as “adult tummy time” on TikTok, the small backbend encourages spinal alignment while opening your chest and shoulders. Plus, the natural lumbar extension results in a gentle, yet effective, stretch for your lower back.

Going upside down—even in subtle ways—is medicine for your body and mind. Lifting your hips in Bridge Pose does exactly that even as it also engages your core, opens your shoulders, and fires up your glutes while giving your lower back a break. This subtle inversion provides a dose of calm while inviting you to adjust as needed—simply lift your hips to your preferred height and hang out for awhile.

If a chiller version of this shape sounds like just what you need after a long week behind the wheel, try sliding a couple pillows, a bolster, or a block beneath your sacrum.

Cinematographers’ Favorite Festival Is All About AI This Year — but Artists Still Hate the Tech

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

During the opening ceremony of the 34th ToruńCAMERIMAGE International Film Festival on Saturday, November 7, audience members saw a human replaced by a machine right in front of their eyes. The festival’s founder and artistic director Marek Żydowicz was invited to the stage, only for a robot to walk out in his place and begin addressing the audience and thanking sponsors.

Żydowicz took the stage a few moments later and joked that none of us are immune to the threat of AI and robots coming for our jobs. (The machine was Edward Warchocki, a humanoid robot “influencer” who has gone viral in Poland for videos in which it chases wild boars around Warsaw.) The stunt set a fitting tone for a festival that has devoted this edition to covering the film industry’s most controversial topic. Warchocki remained on stage for much of the opening ceremony, later joined by an actor in an “Inland Empire” rabbit costume who helped tee up an AI-generated video in which the rabbit symbolically opened the festival gates to the new technology. Anyone hoping for a boring, consensus-driven film festival immediately had their expectations adjusted. Related Stories ‘You Can See Everything’ Sweeps Critics Choice Documentary Awards Box Office: ‘You Can See Everything’ Hits $80 Million, Becoming A24’s Third-Biggest Domestic Release

A festival devoted to the technological craft of filmmaking is a fitting place for these discussions. ToruńCAMERIMAGE (formerly known as Camerimage before changing its name to include its Polish host city) is an annual homecoming for cinematographers. Directors of photography are treated like rockstars at the festival, and the entire profession makes an effort to descend upon Toruń each November to discuss their craft and party late into the night. The main competition screenings are crucial to the Best Cinematography Oscar race, with so much of the voting branch in one room. Panels and Q&As often focus on the specific technical details that the overwhelmingly below-the-line audience wouldn’t hear at other festivals. The job of a cinematographer is uniquely positioned at the intersection of art and technology, and the festival was right to assume that its audience would have a lot of thoughts about the topic.

ToruńCAMERIMAGE 2026 is taking a pluralistic approach to the subject by giving AI advocates and opponents equal time, and the programming appears more interested in stirring the pot and sparking discussion than taking a firm position. The festival gave an honorary award to Darren Aronofsky, who has founded an AI studio that works with Google DeepMind, yet also programmed Luca Guadagnino’s biting Sam Altman satire “Artificial” as its opening night film. The opening ceremony was interspersed with video clips featuring the likes of Aronofsky, Steven Spielberg, Gal Gadot, and Ben Affleck sharing views on AI that ranged from optimistic to paranoid. The message was clear: All opinions are welcome, but we can’t put off talking about AI.

Broadly speaking, directors have been more open to the technology than cinematographers — before “Artificial” screened, Guadagnino stressed that his film was driven more by opposition to the consolidation of wealth and power in a few individuals than AI itself. He explained that he simply sees AI as a neutral tool, though he described its current filmmaking applications as a “cheap” trend that he expects to fade with time in the same way that 3D did. And while Aronofsky avoided the topic in his award acceptance speech, a video message from him played during the opening ceremony saw him repeating his mantra that film has always been a technology-driven medium and AI is simply the latest tech innovation that storytellers need to embrace.

But the cinematographers in attendance have not shied away from the bleak reality that the technology is creating. The term “we’re fucked” has been thrown around a lot, and audience questions have been fiery. The program is filled with panels about AI’s influence on cinematography, many of which focus on the rise of “greybox” filmmaking. The technique, notably employed on Doug Liman’s upcoming “Bitcoin” movie and rapidly popping up on other projects, involves filming actors on a neutral background before using AI to add all the production design and lighting during post.

Nobody is denying the practicality of the rapidly improving technology, which eliminates logistical problems relating to weather and locations that have plagued filmmakers for as long as the art form has existed. But it fundamentally alters the role of a cinematographer, whose job has always been to use light and composition techniques to create images in camera. Cinematographers speak about directors asking them for the softest, most generic lighting on set — not because it looks the best, but because it provides the blankest canvas for changing things in post. Even if you’re still booking gigs as a cinematographer, are you even doing the same job if you’re no longer lighting each image?

Cinematography has always been an art form that’s easy to recognize but hard to describe. Countless moviegoers have walked out of theaters praising a film’s cinematography while having no idea what a director of photography actually does on set and how that job overlaps with other positions. A cinematographer’s job is to make images beautiful (or ugly, when the script calls for it) with their knowledge of light and camera equipment. But directors can theoretically frame their own shots, gaffers can light them, and colorists play an increasingly large role in shaping the final look of each image. A recurring topic at the festival has been the fear that new technologies could freeze cinematographers out of the process altogether.

At a debate titled “State of the Image,” Polish cinematographer Bartosz Nalazek was initially presented as the most pro-AI voice on the panel. He explained how he recently wrapped a major TV show that used greybox techniques for 40 percent of its scenes. His message was that cinematographers have to embrace and adapt to the technology in order to keep working. After he finished his opening statement, fellow cinematographer Arthur Reinhart, a two-time recipient of the festival’s Golden Frog award, asked a simple question: “Are you enjoying the work?”

Nalazek did not hesitate to respond. “Totally not,” he said. “It’s horrible, I’m brain-dead.” He went on to explain that while hybrid production is technically easier for cinematographers, moving so much of the artistry to post-production means that “an elevated camera operator could do it.”

The topic represents a seismic change to a constant that cinematographers could always take for granted: their status as the most technologically savvy person on set. Even as filmmaking tools shifted from film stock to digital cameras, DPs stayed employed and respected because they knew how to use those tools better than anyone else. Now, for the first time in history, technology threatens to be the force that pushes them out of the decision-making process.

“I think what made cinematographers become who we are is the leverage over technology and the creative input. When we were shooting on film, that leverage was tremendous because nobody knew how to do it except the cinematographers,” Nalazek said. “What worries me is that we have this feeling that our position is undoubtable and unchangeable. We have this feeling that the director should respect us, the producer should respect us. I think running on that thought is pretty dangerous. We’re kind of in a time where we have to prove our worth. And that’s sad.”

One advantage of having these conversations at such a technically-oriented festival is that the cinematographers are also honest about what AI isn’t doing. Criticisms are often preempted with the caveat that AI is a broad, loaded term — anyone who edits in DaVinci or uses biometric functions on cameras has technically been using AI tools for years. Cinematographers who have worked on greybox sets make it clear that hybrid AI films are not made by one guy typing prompts in a room by himself. It can be true that AI has shrunk crew sizes enough for the community to feel strained while still acknowledging that these projects often employ hundreds of traditional film workers. And speakers have at times pushed back against audience questions that blame all AI adoption on strawman arguments about greedy producers and execs who could afford to pay artists but simply don’t want to. But nuance doesn’t make the problem any less existential.

Nobody knows where things are going next, but there’s a deep sense that every option and idea should be on the table right now. Some cinematographers are making peace with the notion that traditional filmmaking will become a specialized niche product, akin to artisanal pottery or handmade furniture, rather than the norm for everyday entertainment. We’ll be making a luxury product for a smaller audience willing to pay for it, and we’ll have to charge them accordingly, the thinking goes. Others have floated the idea that the on-set cinematography work of setting up shots will become a lower-level, commoditized job. Directors will still need masterful artists like Roger Deakins to advise them on lighting and image composition, but that high-level work could become more of a post-production role.

The only idea that nobody is taking seriously is that there’s anything cinematographers can do to put the AI genie back in the bottle.

“Camerimage is a place where all the cinematographers from around the world are coming,” Reinhart said. “If we had a voice, then it should be coming from here, and I hope it will. But I am not very optimistic it will change anything.”

« Je suis candidat à l'élection présidentielle de 2027 » : L'annonce inattendue de Teddy Riner !

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

C'est une annonce pour le moins inattendue dans le monde du sport tricolore. Ce lundi 9 novembre 2026, Teddy Riner a officialisé sa candidature à l'élection présidentielle de 2027 dans une vidéo publiée sur son compte X, tout en lançant son propre mouvement politique intitulé « La France qu'on veut ».

Le judoka français de 37 ans se lance ainsi en politique après avoir dominé le judo international. Quintuple champion olympique et onze fois champion du monde, Teddy Riner a bâti un palmarès exceptionnel qui le place au sommet de sa discipline. Alors que son avenir sportif restait au cœur des discussions depuis les Jeux de Paris, le Français surprend en ouvrant un tout autre chapitre personnel. Un virage spectaculaire.

Dans une vidéo publiée ce lundi 9 novembre 2026 sur son compte X, Teddy Riner a pris la parole face caméra afin de lever le voile sur ses intentions pour la prochaine présidentielle. Le judoka de 37 ans a officialisé sa démarche avec des mots simples et directs.

Conscient des réactions que son entrée en politique peut susciter, le quintuple champion olympique a tenu à devancer immédiatement les critiques concernant son manque d'expérience dans les fonctions publiques. Teddy Riner a ainsi déclaré : « Je sais ce qu'on va dire de moi. Il est fou, il n'est pas du sérail, il n'a pas les codes, il n'a jamais fait de politique. Mais posez-vous la question avec moi, qu'est-ce qui est fou aujourd'hui ? Je n'ai pas passé ma vie derrière un public, mais le combat a toujours fait partie de moi. » Une détermination affichée.

Pour concrétiser ses ambitions, le judoka français de 37 ans a également fondé sa propre formation politique ce lundi 9 novembre 2026, baptisée « La France qu'on veut ». Cette prise de parole sur les réseaux sociaux a d'ailleurs été accompagnée d'un entretien accordé au quotidien Le Parisien pour préciser le sens de sa démarche. Dans cet échange, Teddy Riner revendique une volonté de rassemblement sans affiliation partisane, affirmant vouloir devenir un « président qui fédère » sans se situer à droite ou à gauche.

En effet, le champion tricolore a expliqué que sa décision avait mûri dans le sillage direct de l'élan collectif suscité par les Jeux olympiques de Paris 2024. Porté par cette dynamique populaire, il entend désormais transposer cette ferveur sur le plan politique. Ainsi, son mouvement politique refuse d'entrer dans les clivages traditionnels pour privilégier une adhésion commune à l'échelle du pays. Un projet ambitieux.

Toutefois, cette ambition politique ne pousse pas Teddy Riner vers la retraite sportive. Sur le plan des compétitions, sa dernière apparition individuelle remonte aux Jeux olympiques de Paris en août 2024, suivie d'une participation sous les couleurs du PSG Judo en Ligue des champions en décembre 2024. Malgré cette nouvelle démarche politique, le quintuple champion olympique et onze fois champion du monde de judo demeure bel et bien un athlète en activité.

Au-delà de l'élection présidentielle de 2027, Teddy Riner a ainsi fait part de son souhait de participer aux Jeux olympiques de Los Angeles 2028 si ses futures responsabilités et les Français le lui permettent. Le judoka français lie donc son avenir sportif à la suite de son engagement public, avec en ligne de mire les Jeux olympiques de Los Angeles 2028.

EU sues IBM, Atos and Leonardo over border system delays, FT reports

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

EU agency eu-LISA is taking the three firms to court over late delivery of its Entry/Exit System for travellers.

The European Union has sued IBM, French IT group Atos and Italian defence company Leonardo over years of delays in developing its new biometric border system, the Financial Times reported on Monday.

The case was brought by eu-LISA, the EU agency responsible for large-scale IT systems used in border management and security. According to its annual accounts, published in July, the agency filed the lawsuit in Estonia in December 2025, where it is based.

A spokesperson for Harju County Court, Estonia’s largest district court, confirmed to the FT that the civil case had been filed there.

No hearings have yet taken place or been scheduled.

The dispute concerns the Entry/Exit System (EES), which records the fingerprints and photographs of non-EU travellers when they first enter the Schengen area, covering 29 countries. The system is designed to identify people who overstay their permitted time or are wanted by authorities.

Originally scheduled to launch in 2022, the EES only became fully operational in April this year. Its rollout was also plagued by technical problems, with faulty fingerprint scanners contributing to long queues at European airports over the summer.

eu-LISA told the FT that the legal dispute had “no impact on the functioning of the Entry/Exit System (EES), which is fully operational across Europe”. The European Commission said the case concerned delays during the system’s development, rather than its current operation.

In 2019, eu-LISA awarded the three companies a €142m contract to build the system’s core infrastructure, according to earlier reports. The contract’s value increased to €212m in 2021, the FT reported.

Correspondence seen by the newspaper shows that the agency and the contractors had been arguing over missed deadlines and financial penalties for several years. In October 2021, Atos Belgium and IBM Belgium challenged a fine, citing external factors including the Covid-19 pandemic. In February 2022, all three companies objected to revised deadlines and criticised the agency’s management.

The contract expired last year, and a new group of companies has since taken over the work. However, eu-LISA said Atos, Leonardo and IBM remained responsible for the project until August.

A 2024 Bloomberg investigation found that agency documents attributed much of the delay to Atos. The FT subsequently reported that the company had used its Moscow office to purchase software for the system. eu-LISA said it had found no security breaches.

Leonardo and Atos declined to comment to the FT, and IBM did not respond to the newspaper’s request for comment.

How Wuthering Waves Drives the Next Evolution of Anime Action RPGs

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Fast combat, fluid traversal and Resonators who fight like themselves: how Wuthering Waves keeps raising the bar for anime action RPGs.

This article was produced in collaboration with our sponsor, Kuro Games.

When Wuthering Waves launched in May 2024, it arrived as one more open-world anime action RPG in a crowded field. Two years later, Version 3.7, "Prism's Illusion, Heart's Illumination", went live on September 30, 2026, closing the Xuanfang arc and adding two new Resonators, Hsin and Suoming. Kuro Games' title now sets the pace for the genre.

That reputation rests on four pillars: fast-paced combat, fluid movement and exploration, environments that keep evolving with every region, and compelling character presentation. Each one is strong on its own, but they also feed into each other and into the story.

In this article, we look at how these pillars connect, using examples from launch through Version 3.7, and why together they have raised what players expect from an anime action RPG. If you have never set foot on Solaris-3, there has never been a better time to start.

In Wuthering Waves, a weapon type is only the starting point. Every Resonator is officially classed as a Sword, Broadblade, Pistols, Gauntlets or Rectifier user, yet most of them fight with something that is uniquely theirs:

Roccia is a Gauntlets user, and her combat style is made even more distinctive by fighting alongside Pero, the mysterious magic box that serves as both prop and partner. Even her Intro Skill is called “Pero, Help.”

Aemeath is classed as a Sword user, but her kit revolves around switching into a mech form rooted in her past as an Exostrider Synchronist. The mech has its own attack chain, while Sync Strike: Armament Merge brings her two combat styles together.

Hsin is a Rectifier user who shifts between Answering Form and Illumining Form, with different attacks and action modules making transformation part of her rotation rather than a visual flourish.

The wider combat system supports that individuality. Players dodge, counter, use Echo Skills, manage each Resonator’s Forte Circuit, and switch characters in the middle of a combo. When Concerto Energy is ready, a switch can activate the outgoing Resonator’s Outro Skill and the incoming Resonator’s Intro Skill, turning a character change into a coordinated handoff of damage, buffs, healing, or positioning.

The basic rhythm is easy to understand, but there is room for more technical play. Experienced players can use what the community calls “swap-cancel,” switching during eligible animations to shorten rotations or layer actions from multiple Resonators. High-difficulty encounters further reward players for learning enemy patterns, judging counter windows, and deciding when to press an attack or dodge away. The same team can therefore feel simple in casual play and much more demanding when optimized.

Because combat moves so quickly, presentation must remain readable. Clear enemy wind-ups, distinctive intro animations, impact pauses, sound, and camera movement all help communicate what is happening. Hsin’s Answering and Illumining forms illustrate that balance: the transformation creates spectacle, but it also changes the structure and rhythm of her rotation. The visual climax and the mechanical decision are the same moment.

The attack style goes one step further: it tells you who the character is. Roccia's acrobatic flips and showy gestures come from her life as First Mate of the Fool's Troupe, a traveling performer of Rinascita's carnival scene. Aemeath’s singing carries her love of music into battle, while her mech form recalls her past as an Exostrider Synchronist.

The strongest character designs do more than look distinctive. Their animation, resources, and rotations all express the same personality, giving players another way to understand each Resonator through the rhythm of controlling them.

Wuthering Waves makes moving around Solaris-3 feel good, and that comfort is what lets the environment shine. Its foundation already includes sprinting, wall-running, climbing, gliding, and the Grapple. Later updates expanded that vocabulary:

Version 2.0 introduced Flight, giving the player a faster and more flexible way to move through Rinascita’s vertical spaces.

Rinascita’s transport Gondolas make Ragunna’s canals part of its traversal network. In Version 2.1’s Old Man and the Sea fishing activity, the Rover can also steer the Gondola Rachel through the waters around the Riccioli Islands.

Version 3.0 introduced the Expedition Motorbike, a system closely associated with the long routes and open terrain of Lahai-Roi.

Version 3.6 introduced sword-riding flight at specific quest locations in Xuanfang, giving the new region its own form of aerial traversal.

With travel this smooth, the journey becomes part of the fun rather than the gap between destinations. Instead of trudging toward the next waypoint, you sweep over rooftops and ridgelines, and you start noticing what lies below.

In Wuthering Waves, traversal is not bolted onto the map; the map design is inspired by it.

Rinascita’s canals, bridges, and rooftops work in harmony with Flight, helping the city feel like one connected space rather than a maze of streets. Lahai-Roi’s wide-open terrain offers a similarly natural fit for the motorbike. Roads connect resource points, while racetracks, motorbike challenges, and the ability to gather materials while riding make driving more than a way to get around; they make it part of exploration itself. Traversal and environment complement each other, reflecting a broader design approach that considers the player experience as a whole.

This relationship matters because the way players move changes how they read the landscape. A fast or flexible traversal system encourages exploration and detours: a rooftop becomes a route, a distant ridge becomes an invitation, and a vertical structure becomes something to circle rather than merely climb.

The environment stops being a backdrop for objectives and becomes space players come to understand through movement.

The environment is also full of puzzles, and each region solves them in its own way. Lahai-Roi has its Smartprint Cube puzzles, Echo-form puzzles ask you to transform into an Echo to reach a solution, and ice-cube puzzles have you shifting blocks to open the way forward. Other activities use movement, object manipulation, or region-specific rules to redirect the player away from the main path.

The rewards vary, but many chests, exploration milestones, and activities contribute Astrite or other progression materials. More importantly, the puzzles reward curiosity itself, encouraging players to follow detours, investigate anything unusual, and explore beyond the main route.

Good traversal gets the player to a location. Good environmental design rewards them for looking closer.

Connecting story and gameplay means more than placing a battle between two cutscenes. Narrative, combat, level-design, and animation teams must agree early on what a character or chapter is fundamentally about. When that alignment works, dialogue, combat rotations, puzzles, and the environment can all speak the same design language.

Kuro Games builds this alignment around each character’s core concept. Cartethyia’s “three swords as one” is reflected in both her story and combat identity, while the sun-and-moon imagery surrounding Augusta and Luno connects their identity to their battle presentation. Frequent communication between teams allows an idea discovered in one area to echo in another, whether players encounter it first in a quest or in a character’s kit.

Version 2.2 provides a clear example. The Rover accompanies Cartethyia through a series of gameplay sequences in the Inverted Tower before finally reaching Fleurdelys. Here, the gameplay is closely integrated with Cartethyia’s abilities and story. The concept of her three swords is echoed in an objective that asks players to set three swords into three locations across Avinoleum to lift the final seal. The battle sequence involving Cadanto likewise reflects the guardian-knight bond between Cartethyia and Cadanto, while the Inverted Tower design gives physical form to the opposing aspects represented by Cartethyia and Fleurdelys. Together, these elements give the entire sequence a unified feel.

Making that integration work also requires careful control of pacing. Main Quests cannot always play like endgame challenges, because excessive difficulty can interrupt a scene’s emotional flow. By emphasizing interaction, feedback, and narrative expression, the gameplay supports the story rather than competing with it. Character mechanics express identity, objectives turn lore into action, and architecture gives relationships a physical form. The result is not merely a story interrupted by gameplay, but a story the player helps perform.

What makes Wuthering Waves stand out is not one great update but a steady run of them. Across 23 version releases since launch, Kuro Games has delivered more than 100 hours of main-story content, over 200 square kilometers of open-world maps, 206 distinct enemy types, and 67 playable Resonators. Every major version has pushed the world and its possibilities a little further.

The clearest example was this summer’s Cyberpunk: Edgerunners collaboration. It brought Lucy and Rebecca into Wuthering Waves alongside two Cyberpunk-themed areas, Night City and the Moon, and a Nightmare: Adam Smasher boss encounter. Its emotional coda led many fans to nickname it “Edgerunners Episode 11.” CD Projekt Red associate game director Paweł Sasko summed up the response: “We have done a bunch of collabs in the past, small and huge, and I have never seen a response this insane from the community.”

Version 3.4, which featured the collaboration, also set a new Steam concurrent-player peak for Wuthering Waves. Across 2026, the game repeatedly reached No. 1 on Steam’s Top Sellers charts in major markets, and its latest update, Version 3.7, continued that performance by taking the global top spot on launch day. That momentum has translated into formal recognition as well: a Still Playing – Mobile nomination at the 2026 Golden Joystick Awards, plus Best Ongoing Game and Popular Vote at the Thailand Game Awards, where Kuro Games was also named Best Game Publisher; and three nominations at the 17th Hollywood Music in Media Awards in 2026, across Song – Video Game (Console & PC), Song/Score – Mobile Video Game, and Live Concert for Visual Media.

With Version 3.8 now approaching, Kuro Games is not slowing down. For anyone wondering where the anime action RPG goes next, Solaris-3 is a good place to look. Kuro has done it again—and the next step is already on the way.

My Life as an Asylum Seeker in the U.S.

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

I came here 11 years ago to give my daughter a better life. I believe in the American dream, even if the American government doesn’t want me here.

I want to tell you about some things that I think you can only understand when you are an immigrant.

The first, and most important, is that emigrating is not something you do without a reason to leave. I will tell you mine. In Venezuela, when I would take my daughter to school, she had to ride lying down on the floor of the car. At any moment, someone might knock on your door, point a gun, steal your phone. It was safer to have her flat on the ground, below the windows. She was a great student at a private school, and that is what it took to get her there. I challenge you to imagine being a father and seeing that little girl, not even ten years old, huddling for safety.

That was my reason for leaving, but it is not the reason I and my family have been allowed to stay here. We are asylum seekers, and there are strict rules around qualifying for asylum in the United States. The fear of being mugged is not enough. But political persecution is. In the year after the 2013 election of President Nicolás Maduro, when life in Venezuela was very difficult, the country erupted in protests. I was among the protesters, and I was detained and separated from my family for a week. We decided that when we visited my sister in Miami in 2015, we would request asylum.

This is surely something you only understand as an immigrant: the specific, and confusing, rules and requirements and procedures of the American immigration system. People who are born here can take these things for granted. They don’t understand how much of your life can be dictated by paperwork and how the paperwork your life most depends on always has the most boring name. When we got to the United States, the application for asylum I filled out and turned in to U.S. Citizenship and Immigration Services, a part of the Department of Homeland Security, was called Form I-589.

According to the government, it should have taken at most five months for the application to be processed and to get an interview, which would allow USCIS to evaluate if my family should be granted asylum.

Our wait began when I turned in that form. That was the start of the first countdown clock. You think about it every day, because for those five months, you cannot work. When they are up, you can fill out another form, Form I-765, to apply for a work permit. But in the meantime: Five months… four months… three months…

We were fortunate: We had savings. Miami was expensive, but we were getting by.

As the clock ticked down, we had a decision to make. We had family in Miami, but it was too expensive for us to stay for the long term. If the process dragged on much longer, we’d run out of money. We decided to move north, to Orlando. We didn’t know anyone there, but it was affordable. That’s where we would wait out our chance at the American dream.

When we moved there, the newest iPhone was the iPhone 6s. A nuclear deal was finalized with Iran. Barack Obama was president.

Eleven years later, we are still waiting for an interview.

Right now, when many people here think about immigration, they think about ICE—men in the streets with guns, killing people in Minnesota, or Maine, or Texas. I worry about ICE every day, but what has defined the past decade of my life is the waiting. Maybe if I can tell you about it, you will understand something, even a small part, of what it is to be an immigrant.

In Orlando, we were alone. We moved in December, a difficult month. Christmas is a big holiday in Venezuela, and there the family is always together: cousins, siblings, uncles, aunts, grandparents. Our first Christmas was just the three of us.

I dutifully filled out Form I-765. I understood the work permit would take about two weeks to arrive. Once I had it, another countdown: It would be good only for one year. In that time, though, I could get a job and also a driver’s license, which would make our lives much easier. And surely, we thought, a year was enough time for our interview appointment to come through.

Instead of two weeks, the permit took three months. Three more months of stretching our savings. There were desperate moments. We sometimes had to go to churches to get food so we wouldn’t starve. We wondered if we should go home, live with the risks in Venezuela. Finding resolve in these moments is hard. Having a young child motivates you, but you also see the challenges they face. My daughter was nine years old when we arrived. She had anxiety attacks when she first started school, which was in a different language and organized much differently from her school in Venezuela.

When my work permit finally arrived, we were so happy. This card with the faded flag and my picture and the red border—we felt that we could sustain ourselves.

It wasn’t so easy. My English is not perfect. It isn’t now, and it really wasn’t when I started looking for jobs. I’m an engineer by training. I worked at a telephone company in Venezuela. When I first started working here in the United States, I worked overnight, I cleaned bathrooms, I cleaned kitchens, I washed cars. I’m not trying to denigrate those jobs; they were the first jobs that I could find. But I could not get paid well doing them. After we had been here for a year, our savings ran out. We were lucky to have family and friends who could help us. But we kept going to food pantries. I always tried to make sure that my daughter and my wife did not go without food. I often didn’t eat enough myself.

After we had been here for a few years, my mom came to the United States to visit me and my sister. It was the first time she had seen me in four years. I was so skinny she cried.

I did some English classes online, but they did not really work. Here is another thing you understand when you are an immigrant: When you have to start to speak on the street, it’s very different from the lessons that they teach you in class. Really, construction job sites were my classroom. With more English, I became more confident I could find a job in my field.

Before I could do that, my work permit expired. I applied for a renewal, and I got another one. Another year. Another countdown until the day I could, hopefully, make my family’s case.

By then, President Obama had left office, but before he did his administration made work permits valid for two years. That was what my next renewal got me—a little more time to work, and save, and wait. But it also made me think the government might expect us to be waiting two more years.

Meanwhile, Donald Trump had been elected president. I wasn’t blind to his attacks on immigrants, but my family and I were following the rules, working hard, just trying to survive. And Venezuelans, historically, hadn’t immigrated that much to the United States. We didn’t think the attacks were about us.

But things were changing. More and more people were coming from Venezuela and from other countries in South and Central America. My family thought of ourselves as three people looking for safety, but we could tell we were becoming something different: a problem.

We started to see news that the people who were waiting for asylum interviews were waiting four years. Later, it became five years. And then, as you started to talk to people, they tell you that they’ve been waiting six years or they’ve been waiting seven years.

It was hard to imagine how we could possibly last that long, but in 2019 I saw a listing for a job in my old profession. It was with a major telecommunications company. I was hired to join a team that climbs up telecom towers to do maintenance and upgrades. Back in Venezuela, I had supervised this kind of work.

My daughter was in high school by this time, and I would think about her when I was up there. When she was little, she would tell me she wanted to be an engineer. I told her, You don’t have to be an engineer just because I’m an engineer. It could be scary being up so high. We have harnesses, and something like parachutes, and we’re always connected to something in case we fall, but we recognize that we can’t get too comfortable, because any error could end our lives. Still, there was something special about being able to see for miles, this place that held my family’s future stretching out in every direction.

Most of us on the crew were new, many of us immigrants. Our boss was impressed at the quality of our work. He asked us once, How is it that you guys are able to do this kind of work not having done it before? I told him we were trained. And also that when you have this need to survive, you have this need to get ahead, you end up finding a way.

For years we had been saving, and Covid made it possible for us to save much more. The pandemic, strangely, was good for my family. I was an essential worker, and I received bonuses and other incentives. I never stopped working. At the same time, Covid exposed the political problems I protested in Venezuela, which made the pandemic even worse there. It was a reminder of why we had left.

And the house was a reminder of home. In Venezuela, people move less than they do here. Neighbors stay for years, for a very long time. The importance of a house there, like here, is that when you own it, no one can remove you from it. It really meant something to feel that feeling in America—like we were beginning to live the American dream. My daughter, in her way, had even become a typical American teenager. Our new house was about forty minutes from where we had been living, so she had to change schools. She sulked.

In 2023, the Biden administration extended work permits from two years to five years. I think probably they did this because, after the pandemic, everything was chaos. I knew it was a sign that my family might be waiting unimaginably longer for our interview, but when I renewed my permit in 2024, after living life in a five-month increment, then one-year increments, then two-year increments, five years felt like forever, like a clock it was hard to imagine reaching zero.

I won’t tell you that it all came crashing down when President Trump was elected a second time. It’s not just that Orlando wasn’t like Minnesota. There was no crash. It was a gradual decline in freedom and a gradual increase in fear.

Simply, here is what began to happen this past December and January, when things got bad: You’re driving on the highway and you’re followed by an undercover cop. Sometimes it’s for a few minutes, sometimes it’s for ten minutes or more. Sometimes they pull you over. Sometimes they detain you.

In Florida, many law-enforcement agencies collaborate with ICE, so almost anywhere you go is a risk. I drive a lot for work, and I see enforcement in exits and rest stops and Wawas, in school areas where you have to go slow. There are police or highway patrol or border patrol on the turnpike down to Miami, and on State Road 417, and on Interstate 4. You can be referred to ICE if you owe money for a toll, or if you don’t stop for a stop sign, or if they see that your driver’s license says that it’s temporary. Unlike most people, I like working more than fifty miles from home—it means I can stay in a hotel, which means less driving.

Going to work, or for essentials, is nearly all we do. When my family needs groceries, only my wife goes or only I go. We can’t risk both being picked up, because of my daughter. As for my daughter, she sometimes has my sister—a lawful permanent resident who moved to Orlando after we did—drive her to school.

There are a lot of people in my situation in this part of Florida, so we find ways to help each other. We have chat groups, maybe fifteen hundred people total, that warn each other about high-risk areas. And I am a member of an organization called the Asylum Seeker Advocacy Project that has helped us understand our rights and how to be safe.

Thank God, I have never been stopped. But my wife and I each keep a binder in the car just in case. We know it might not be worth a lot, but we carry it with us. It contains all of our paperwork, the forms with boring names that are proof we have a right to be here. Our immigration paperwork, our asylum applications, the approvals for work permits that we’ve gotten before, the asylum receipts and claims. Everything we might need in case one of us is detained. You have to basically see if the documentation convinces the police officer to let you go. But it is up to luck. It is up to God.

Sometimes a risk is worth it to feel that you are still living.

Once, I took a trip to a bar with a few friends. Three beers and an Uber home. It was so scary, but we did it.

The gym, four days a week, at 4:30 a.m.—before law enforcement is on the road. Once or twice a week, when her schedule allows, my daughter comes. She has the certifications to be a personal trainer, so she’s my personal trainer. There comes an age, though, at which children don’t really want to spend that much time with their parents, so she just tells me what to do and goes to do her own exercise. I still like having her there. I’ve lost about forty-three pounds, roughly twenty kilos, over the last year and a half. This time by choice.

Then there was the World Baseball Classic, in Miami last March. I love baseball. When Venezuela reached the semis, I knew I had to go. We left at 3:00 a.m. for the four-hour drive down. Venezuela beat Italy in the semis, so we stayed for the final. Of all the teams we could have faced, it turned out to be the U.S.A. The game was in the evening, and it ended late in the night. We drove directly back to Orlando.

Venezuela ended up champions, by the way. It was like vengeance, because the United States knocked us out in the quarterfinals of the last WBC, in 2023. And to top it off, my favorite player, Wilyer Abreu, hit a home run in the championship game. He’s from Maracaibo, just like me.

We have an ongoing joke with the people we know who are also in limbo. We’ll casually refer to “when we were treated like U.S. citizens.” Now we’re treated like criminals, but in other times, in other administrations, we could make a life without worrying so much, the way that we do now. Still, my family does our best to live like U.S. citizens. We follow the rules. I would not have a job, for example, if Immigration had not given me a work permit with which to do that job. I know, because for eight months, I did not work—I went hungry. We pay taxes on our house. I pay taxes on what I make at my job. I’ve always paid. I’ve been here eleven years now, making a life, paying taxes to the government, but I still am here with no status. Who is the criminal?

I guess it’s not really a joke. I can tell you that if you were an immigrant, you would understand.

Since things got really bad in December and January, they haven’t gotten much worse, but they also haven’t gotten better. We stick to our routine: work, groceries, school.

Recently, one of the places that I was going to have to service for my job was a police station, and I told my boss that I could not do it. I told him that he should change me out for a different job, and that if he didn’t I would stay at home until I could be assigned to a different site. He was able to find something safer for me to do. He is a very understanding man. Several of us are in the same situation, and the company has been very supportive.

So many people down here are like that. In my experience, the politicians do not make a country. The people do.

And so through everything, my feelings toward this country have not changed. I will stand on the fact that this is a great country. I’m very thankful. The situation in Venezuela was not good. Some people tell me that things are better there now, since the U.S. detained President Maduro. I’m not hopeful. I am grateful for the peace we’ve had here. I came for a better life for my family and for my daughter. We bought our house; we have saved for our daughter’s future.

Eleven years is far too long to live someplace and still be made to feel like an outsider, let alone like a criminal. That is the thinking of someone who takes home for granted. But I am an immigrant. One thing I understand is how to extend grace to a place that is foreign to me. I have nothing against the U.S. I am grateful for this country.

At the end of last year, for the first time since I have lived here, the government made work permits shorter. Now they last only eighteen months. The wait has not gotten any shorter, though. We’ve seen no signs we’ll get an interview anytime soon. I was very lucky to get a five-year permit when I did. Right now, my countdown runs until 2029.

If there is one thing I am certain Americans who were born here do not understand, it is the immigration system itself. I have been telling you about the past decade of my life and our excruciating wait for our asylum interview. I have not told you yet that that is not the end of the process.

After our interview, we will wait for a decision. Officially, it should take only two weeks. I know it will take longer. It will be a long time still that we live with the question that, for eleven years now, has been hanging over our heads: What if we do not get to stay? But in a way, after all this time building a life here, the uncertainty of that question has lost some of its power. If I’m honest with you, when my daughter is ready to graduate college, I can tell you that I could die in peace, because she’s my only daughter, and what I wanted most for her was a good future.

A long time ago, she changed her mind about being an engineer. After she started taking college classes, she decided to study something called media design. She’s learning how to create applications, like for phones. She graduates in May.

I still cannot believe that when we got here, she was just nine years old. It’s painful for me, as it is for any father, to look nostalgically back at when this grown woman was my little girl. But it also fills me with joy. My wife and I have spent every minute of every hour of every day of all of the time since she was young counting down—to the exhaustion of our savings, to the end of a work permit, to an interview. You persevere through these things for your children for a simple reason. Children should only ever have to count up.

Grant McCann faces biggest test yet at Doncaster Rovers - Leon Wobschall comment

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Last November, Grant McCann watched his side lose 2-1 at Lincoln City - to extend the club's winless run to nine league matches, serious territory for most managers.

Fortunately, McCann could count on having credit in the bank—Rovers' 1,693-strong support sang his name intermittently. That stoic patience was rewarded and Rovers eventually turned a corner.

Did you know with a Digital subscription to Yorkshire Post, you can get access to all of our premium content, as well as benefiting from fewer ads, loyalty rewards and much more.

Which brings us to the present. Rovers' travelling followers - many of whom would have been among the big away contingent at Lincoln - were in far more febrile and angry mood after seeing their side succumb to a pitiful FA Cup exit at Kidderminster Harriers on Saturday.

Damning chants of 'You're not fit to wear the shirt' and 'We turn up and why don't you' arrived. McCann himself got the treatment.

A chorus of 'Sacked in the morning' was aired with McCann booed at the end of an awful afternoon.

Forty-two places separated the two clubs in the pyramid ahead of the first-round tie at Aggborough. What made the 3-0 defeat all the more stark for Rovers was the fact that Harriers, mid-table in the National League, had won just once in their previous nine matches in all competitions.

And then there was what had happened seven days earlier when Doncaster again imploded en route to a wretched 6-1 reverse at an Aaron Collins-inspired MK Dons. McCann's side are currently just one place above the League One drop zone.

After Kidderminster, McCann did not shy away from his responsibilities - as he never does - and fronted up. For the second successive Saturday, he issued an apology to fans.

In his time at Rovers, McCann has been in tough situations before and come out on the other side. The Northern Irishman has consistently spoken about how he actually revels in being written off and uses it as fuel.

“I’m from Sandy Row in Belfast. I do not hide,” is something he has said on more than one occasion.

The break in Rovers' league schedule might just be timely. McCann and co must close ranks and come out fighting ahead of a critical Yorkshire derby double-header against Bradford City and Huddersfield Town. Their previous derby victory against Sheffield Wednesday is something that should at least inspire them.

Taking aside the Trophy trip to Rochdale, Rovers have shipped 12 goals in three matches. McCann brought his players in on a Sunday to go over mistakes following the rout at MK Dons. Worryingly, another dereliction of defensive duty arrived at Kidderminster, where his side lost duels all over the pitch.

Going forward, Rovers have had issues in terms of goalscoring and have yet to get a consistent tune out of several summer signings. Injuries have compounded matters.

McCann will need to draw upon all of his experience to change the narrative at a club he loves. Don't bet against him, but it will be his biggest Rovers test to date.

The fans don't have his back as much as they once did.

McCann enjoys a fight and it's just as well.

Every Martin McDonagh-Directed Movie, Ranked (Including Wild Horse Nine)

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

British-Irish filmmaker Martin McDonagh has become one of cinema's foremost stewards of the tragicomedy. In a McDonagh movie, violence or death can be hilarious, or devastating, or both. He forces us to sympathize with unlikable or even evil characters by showing that even those bad eggs still have human sides. Catholicism casts its shadow over McDonagh's stories, especially in how he loves to focus on guilty, even suicidally guilty, people. He dares you to sympathize with those guilty people in spite of their horrible sins and, quite often, he succeeds.

While there are usually glimmers of hope for redemption, McDonagh's stories never end with tidy answers, either. As for his crackling dialogue, it's remarkable how adept he is at both non-sequiturs and rhythmic back-and-forth. If Quentin Tarantino had been raised in London and Galway instead of Tennessee and Los Angeles, you'd have a writer like Martin McDonagh.

McDonagh began as a playwright, producing several plays in the 1990s and early 2000s. He moved into film in 2004 with his directed short, "Six Shooter," and then jumped to features with 2008's "In Bruges." He's remained there since and has become a frequent Oscar nominee to boot. To celebrate his sixth and latest film "Wild Horse Nine" hitting theaters, here are all of McDonagh's movies, ranked!

McDonagh's first film was a short one: 2004's "Six Shooter," which is available to watch for free on YouTube. In 2006, "Six Shooter" received an Academy Award for Best Live Action Short Film. It's the least of McDonagh's films by its very nature as a short, but running only 26 minutes has a positive double-edge: The script is small but airtight, like a great short story should be.

Brendan Gleeson plays Donnelly, an Irish man taking a train ride after his wife died in the hospital that morning. There's also a couple (David Wilmot and Aisling O'Sullivan) onboard reeling from the death of their infant son, as well as a very rude and loud kid (Rúaidhrí Conroy). The kid enjoys running his mouth and prodding the other passengers, and rest assured, he's holding a dark secret too.

Even with its short runtime, "Six Shooter" still has a lot of what have become McDonagh's trademarks as a storyteller. There's a death scene that'll make you jump with how abrupt it is, as well as an overall theme of grief-driven people looking to suicide for the answer. There's also a twist, seeded offhandedly in the movie's opening, that comes back and completely reframes everything at the end.

Considering the accolades "Six Shooter" earned, all the way up to the Academy, it's also a credit to McDonagh that he hasn't remade it as a full-length feature. The train ride setting benefits from the brevity, and while the twist is satisfying at 20 minutes of build-up, at 90 it might be a drag. "Six Shooter" sits well enough as is.

In "Three Billboards Outside Ebbing, Missouri," McDonagh wrote a story about a tight-knit community in the vein of several of his plays. But as you can guess from the title, this one takes place in small town America, not rural Ireland. Whether McDonagh succeeded in his self-imposed challenge of setting with "Three Billboards Outside Ebbing, Missouri" still splits audiences.

Mildred Hayes (Frances McDormand, who won Best Actress for the performance) is a woman grieving the still-unsolved rape and murder of her daughter, Angela (Kathryn Newton). To apply some pressure to police chief Bill Willoughby (Woody Harrelson), she puts up three billboards outside the town that read, in succession: "Raped While Dying." "And Still No Arrests?" "How Come, Chief Willoughby?" Mildred turns her grief into a spectacle the town can't look away from.

Upon wide release, "Three Billboards Outside Ebbing, Missouri" attracted a vocal backlash alongside its accolades. The main lightning rod was the character Jason Dixon (Sam Rockwell), a racist police officer accused of torturing a Black suspect, who attempts a soulful redemption arc. Many found that approach in poor taste; Dixon doesn't repent his bigotry, but the film insists he has a heart in spite of it.

McDonagh has said he did not intend "Three Billboards" as a message movie about American politics, and he's definitely not the type of writer who uses his characters to deliver moralistic lectures. The easy answer is that McDonagh was a little careless about adding local color to the kind of story he's written about Irish villages before. Mixed with his predilection for asking audiences to sympathize with immoral people, that approach landed the wrong way for many. McDonagh's settings usually feel whimsical, like black comedy fairy tales, and that doesn't quite mesh with politically potent themes.

Two of McDonagh's most frequent leading men are Colin Farrell and Sam Rockwell, but he's only used them together in one movie: his third film, and second feature, "Seven Psychopaths," which is a much stranger, more contemplative movie than its violent-sounding title suggests. McDonagh's most structurally ambitious movie, "Seven Psychopaths" is Spike Jonze and Charlie Kaufman's "Adaptation" by way of Quentin Tarantino.

Farrell plays Marty, a writer's block-afflicted screenwriter working on his new script "Seven Psychopaths." He's also best friends with a criminal, Billy (Rockwell), who gets himself in hot water after kidnapping mob boss Charlie Costello's (Woody Harrelson) dog and sleeping with Costello's girlfriend, Angela (Olga Kurylenko).

As Marty, Billy, and Billy's partner-in-crime Hans (Christopher Walken) drive away fleeing the gangsters, the movie tips its hand. Marty suggests that, in his "Seven Psychopaths" script, "the first hour should be like a perfect set-up for an out-and-out revenge flick." But then the leads should "walk away" and just go out to the dessert and talk like real people. Billy is dumbfounded ("No shootouts?!"), but lo and behold, that's what the actual "Seven Psychopaths" you're watching does.

The movie's meta approach lets McDonagh engage in some amusing self-criticism. "Your women characters are awful," Hans tells Marty. Marty, not even trying to deny it, just answers that "it's a hard world for women." There are also layered interludes demonstrating Marty's ideas for his screenplay, like a former Viet Cong soldier (Long Nguyen) on a vengeance quest.

Your mileage with "Seven Psychopaths" will depend heavily on your tolerance for metafiction. But if you can get on its wavelength, it's a very funny movie with three excellently played leads.

As a filmmaker settles into their career, there's always the risk they'll start going through the motions, i.e. making films that only echo what they've said or accomplished in previous, better films. I mention this because McDonagh's latest film "Wild Horse Nine" has the exact same essential premise as "In Bruges" — two professional murderers on holiday where one has been tasked with killing the other.

In 1973, Chris (John Malkovich) and Lee (Sam Rockwell) are CIA agents working in Santiago, Chile to undermine the country's democracy and bring dictator Augusto Pinochet to power. They take a vacation to Easter Island; Chris is dying of cancer, and he wants Lee to kill him in a peaceful place. Their boss, MJ (Steve Buscemi), is worried because Chris is talking about penning memoirs and sharing the CIA's darkest secrets.

This is both Malkovich and Buscemi's first time working with McDonagh, and both actors take to his dialogue easily. Malkovich is perfectly cast as the airy and impish Chris, just as Buscemi brings his tightly wound Mr. Pink from "Reservoir Dogs" energy to MJ. "Old killer haunted by his past" is nothing new, but Chris isn't broody and self-flagellating even though he is suffering Catholic guilt. He's more like the eccentric grandfather who says what's on his mind because he's aged past caring about consequences.

The first chunk of "Wild Horse Nine" is a little uneventful, as Lee and Chris tour Easter Island and bicker. But the same could be said of "In Bruges," so I should've trusted the process. The movie's emotional arcs and its mysteries come together beautifully in the second half. It's also politically sharper, or at least angrier, than "Three Billboards." If you want to hear how America supposedly "protects democracy" across the world, watch something else.

"Breaking up" with a longtime friend can be just as painful as ending a romantic relationship. Don't believe me? Then live a little, but before that, also go and watch "The Banshees of Inisherin," the definitive and brutal friend breakup movie.

In 1994, McDonagh tried to write a play titled "The Banshees of Inisheer," but couldn't crack it. He hung onto the title, and years later he found a story for which to use it (albeit tweaked to reflect the change from the real Irish island Inisheer to the fictional Inisherin). Set in the 1920s, "Banshees" follows two former lifelong friends, farmer Pádraic (Colin Farrell) and fiddle player Colm (Brendan Gleeson).

Colm decides one day he doesn't want to be friends anymore. Unlike your typical movie feud, there's no real inciting incident. Pádraic is a nice guy, but boring, and so Colm — who's in a midlife crisis and wants to write a musical composition that history will remember — doesn't want to speak to him any more. You can tell they've been friends because of convenience first: It's a small island and they have little else in their lives. Since Pádraic didn't do anything wrong, there's no real way to repair their friendship, and attempts at reconciliation eventually beget revenge.

Farrell and Gleeson's casting carries extra weight considering they starred together in McDonagh's "In Bruges." That film was about their characters' saving each other through their friendship, whereas this one is about them hurting each other as a friendship dissolves. Farrell's puppy dog face is perfect for the spurned and confused Pádraic, while Gleeson's turns his usual jovial presence as cold as Inisherin's ocean winds. The virtual third player is Kerry Condon as Pádraic's sister Siobhán, the exasperated straight woman of Inisherin.

"In Bruges" holds a special place in my film-loving heart. I saw it when I was about 14 years old, and, as a Catholic school alum just beginning to love Quentin Tarantino, I was perfectly primed for it to floor me. I still remember it as a pivotal moment in my journey as a cinephile and it holds up as McDonagh's most effective, perfectly-executed movie.

Ray (Colin Farrell) and his mentor Ken (Brendan Gleeson) are two British-Irish hitmen sent to hide out in a Belgian city, Bruges, and await instruction from their boss Harry (Ralph Fiennes). Ken loves the town's old-fashioned charm and the sight-seeing, but Ray is irritable and makes it clear Bruges is the last place he wants to be.

What seems like a buddy comedy reveals itself as a devastating drama when we find out why the pair are in Bruges. During their previous job (Ray's first), a little boy died as collateral damage. It was an accident, yes, but it still only happened because Ray shot up a priest (Ciarán Hinds) by ambushing him in a confessional. So, yeah, can't imagine the good Lord will be looking too kindly on that! Ray has to rediscover the will to live, and Ken — who'd been assigned by Harry to kill Ray as punishment for killing the kid — is his unlikely guardian angel.

From the character arcs to the tone (which remains frequently funny even after we get the devastating truth), "In Bruges" is a writing masterclass elevated even higher by three perfectly in-tune performances. Ray's redemption was echoed off-screen, too. Colin Farrell taking on such a complex and morally compromised but still empathetic character let him prove what a great actor he is after a rough few years prior.
