While the broader crypto market struggles to find direction, the Pieverse price has taken a different path, extending its rally since October 30. The token has climbed from around $1.00 to $1.66, with its latest daily session up more than 15%. The sustained advance has drawn attention to a relatively lesser-known project operating at the intersection of Web3 payments and the emerging AI-agent economy.

However, the rally raises an important question: what is driving PIEVERSE higher when much of the market remains under pressure?

PIEVERSE is showing strong bullish momentum on the daily chart, with the token trading at $1.6666 after gaining 15.49% in the latest session. The recovery has pushed the price above the 0.618 Fibonacci level at $1.4303, bringing the 0.786 level at $1.7068 into immediate focus. A decisive move above this resistance could open the way towards the $2.0589 level, followed by the extended upside target at $2.5065.

However, the RSI climbed to 82.15, moving well above the 70 threshold and indicating overbought conditions. Although this reflects strong buying pressure, it also increases the possibility of profit-taking or a short-term correction. On the downside, $1.4303 is the first important support to watch, followed by the 0.5 Fibonacci level at $1.2362. Holding above these levels would help preserve the recent recovery structure. For now, bulls need to overcome $1.7068 to sustain the rally, while the elevated RSI suggests that further upside may not come without periods of consolidation.

PIEVERSE’s latest rally is accompanied by a sharp increase in derivatives exposure, with aggregated open interest climbing to $37.49 million. The surge suggests that traders are adding positions as the token advances, potentially amplifying price volatility. Funding rates have also turned positive at 0.0198, indicating that bullish positioning is gaining traction in the perpetual futures market.

However, the CVD readings reveal a more complicated picture. Futures CVD remains deeply negative despite a slight recovery in its latest reading. In contrast, spot CVD has steadily climbed towards ~$3.66 million, suggesting that spot buying pressure has been improving. This divergence indicates that the recent rally is not being supported equally across spot and derivatives markets.

Liquidation data also shows recent activity on both sides, with the latest displayed readings at approximately $123,608 and $26,825. While this reflects increased volatility around the recent advance, it does not yet establish a sustained liquidation-driven short squeeze.

For now, the combination of rising open interest, positive funding and improving spot CVD supports the bullish price action. However, if open interest continues expanding while funding remains elevated, the market could become vulnerable to a leveraged long unwind if PIEVERSE fails to sustain its upward momentum.

Another factor that could influence PIEVERSE’s rally is its scheduled token unlock on November 14. Approximately 33.4 million tokens are expected to enter circulation, representing around 3.3% of the total supply. At the prevailing prices used in earlier estimates, the allocation was valued at roughly $32.5 million.

The additional supply could create selling pressure, particularly after the token’s sharp advance since October 30. Investors and early participants may look to realise profits, potentially increasing volatility around the unlock date. However, the impact will depend on the allocation recipients and whether they decide to sell or retain their tokens.

For now, the upcoming unlock adds another uncertainty to PIEVERSE’s bullish momentum. With the RSI already in overbought territory and derivatives exposure expanding, the market’s ability to absorb additional supply could become an important test of the rally’s strength.

PIEVERSE price has demonstrated considerable strength despite uncertainty across the broader crypto market, supported by rising open interest, positive funding rates and improving spot buying pressure. However, the rally is also showing signs of overheating, with the daily RSI above 80 and leveraged exposure increasing.

The $1.7068 Fibonacci level is the immediate hurdle for buyers. A decisive breakout could extend the recovery towards $2.0589, while a rejection may bring $1.4303 and $1.2362 back into focus. The November 14 token unlock adds another factor that could influence market sentiment.

Skyrim lead designer gives the most succinct summary of Bethesda's design I've ever heard: 'You're not playing my game, I'm making your game'

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Though, to be honest, sometimes I want to play someone else's game.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Skyrim lead designer Bruce Nesmith had a wide-ranging chat with The Examined Game recently, touching upon topics like the importance of institutional knowledge, what he liked about Baldur's Gate 3, and what he wants to see in The Elder Scrolls 6: Sentinel. But he also dropped a pithy maxim (and less pithy explanation) about his—and Bethesda's—approach to design in general. Here's what he said.

"If you look at the history of triple-A studios that do sequels… you'll see the best game in a sequence frequently was able to maintain a vision, and was very player-centric-forward: 'You're not playing my game, I'm making your game' kind of thing.

One of the things I've always felt, and [Bethesda] felt it too [is] once we've made a game, it's not ours anymore. It's yours. I, once upon a time, actually had a developer complain to me that the people playing his game—it wasn't anything Bethesda produced—were playing it wrong.

I immediately told him, 'they're not playing it wrong, they cannot play it wrong, it's their game. It's not yours anymore. However they are choosing to play it is the right way to play it'... If you embrace that on the developer side, you find yourself making all different kinds of content, both artistically and design, and for programming in terms of the systems that are available, so that everyone can play the game the way they want to".

I'm not sure I've ever heard a more succinct summation of the Bethesda school of game design than "You're not playing my game, I'm making your game". Accessibility, openness, a sanding down of edges that may scare off the normies (like ability scores and character classes—gone but not forgotten), and a general refusal to foreclose story branches or quest chains based on player decisions: these things are Bethesda to me.

In this maxim you find all the strengths and weaknesses of the studio's approach to open-world RPG design. On the one hand, the world is your oyster. If a questline is losing your interest, flip to another one. Don't like your build? Convert to something new. Bethesda's worlds are all-you-can-eat buffets and you have precisely zero dietary restrictions. True freedom!

And yet, and yet, to a certain kind of tedious grognard (me), it also leaves everything feeling a touch… limp—colourless in content and utterly unresponsive. You might be the head of every guild in the land and the bloodletter-in-chief of the local murder society, but the world is too shy to reply to it. After all, it can't. If your choices impacted things too hard you might experience a consequence, you might rub up against a rough edge, and then suddenly you might not be playing "your" game anymore.

Antarctica 2026-2027: Expedition Roundup

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Here are the Antarctic expeditions for 2026-27. There will be more, as a few still scramble to raise the money or choose to keep their plans to themselves until the last minute. Because of the long charter flights, an expedition to Antarctica, even a “tourist” Last Degree trip, costs close to $100,000 — more, if you want to do something unusual. These are the journeys announced so far. Skiers hit the ice later this month.

Hercules Inlet is the common starting point for South Pole skiers. Skiers need to travel around 1,130km, averaging around 20km per day.

Brit Nathan Lumb originally planned a fully unsupported round-trip to the South Pole but scaled back due to “expedition logistics and funding realities.” He will now ski solo and unsupported from Hercules Inlet to the South Pole. Lumb previously completed a crossing of Norway’s Finnmarksvidda with guiding company Ousland Colliard.

Indian Neeraj Choudhary also trained with Ousland Colliard, crossing the Greenland Ice Sheet in 2024. He hopes to become the second Indian to ski solo and unsupported to the South Pole, following Satish Gogineni, who became the first in 2024-2025.

Satish Gogineni at the South Pole in 2025. Photo: Satish Gogineni

American Lucas Czarnecki will also ski solo and unsupported and claims he will livestream every moment of his journey: “What sets South Signal [his expedition name] apart from other polar expeditions is my commitment to bringing you along for every moment of this extreme adventure. Each day, I will be live-streaming my experience directly from the ice, sharing the raw reality of life on the polar plateau.”

Another American, Meghan Buchanan, will also tackle the route. She plans to start early, leaving for Antarctica on November 12.

Finally, German Dirk Langer will also attempt the route unsupported.

A Berkner Island start means a few extra kilometers, with skiers required to cover around 1,400km to the South Pole.

Max Worsley of the UK and Martin Nesse of Norway also plan to start in early November, but from the edge of Berkner Island. Max is the son of Henry Worsley, who died following an Antarctic expedition in 2015-2016. Max intends to follow much of his father’s final route.

At least two expeditions will begin at the Messner start, 911km from the South Pole. James Garnett of the UK and Norwegian Marius Rehn will each ski solo and unsupported.

Satish Gogineni will be back in Antarctica this year, hoping to build upon his Hercules Inlet experience. Gogineni’s 2026 plan is more ambitious: a solo, 2,000km journey from the outer edge of the Filchner–Ronne Ice Shelf to the inner edge of the Ross Ice Shelf via the South Pole. Gogineni is billing this as a “world-first human achievement” and the “longest solo traverse ever completed on the Antarctic continent.”

If successful, his route would indeed be longer than the truncated crossings of Colin O’Brady and Lou Rudd in 2018. However, his distance would still be well short of Borge Ousland’s 1997 expedition. Ousland became the first person to cross Antarctica solo and unsupported, though he occasionally used a rudimentary handheld kite known as a ski sail. Ousland skied for 65 days and covered 2,845km. At the time, sails were not considered “support.”

Last year, O’Brady tried a significantly longer expedition than his 2018 effort, attempting to ski from the edge of the Ross Ice Shelf to the tip of Berkner Island on the edge of the Ronne Ice Shelf, a total distance of over 2,800km. He eventually ended his crossing attempt after 100 days, with roughly 40% of his total distance remaining.

A map by PECS (the Polar Expeditions Classification Scheme) showing previous solo Antarctic crossing routes. Photo: PECS

Gogineni’s main challenge might be the weight of his sled. With so much weight spread over two sleds, O’Brady was forced to double carry in soft snow on the Ross Ice Shelf last year. A double carry involved shuttling half of his gear forward, then returning to pick up the rest. This meant he had to cover 15km for every 5km of progress. Gogineni says his sled will weigh 181kg.

Mardi Philips, Ida Olsson, and Nia Owens are calling themselves the Antarctic Valkyries and aim to become the “first all-female team to reach the South Pole of Inaccessibility.” They will start at Thorshammer Station (a spot near the Russian Novolazarevskaya Station in Queen Maud Land) and plan to kite-ski 2,500km, first aiming for the Pole of Inaccessibility (the middle of the Antarctic continent), and then continuing to their finish point at the South Pole.

The Antarctic Valkyries proposed route. Photo: Antarctic Valkyries

A Spanish scientific expedition will cross Antarctica by a new route in a unique wind-powered vehicle.

Part of the wind-sled involves a series of tents. Photo: La Vanguardia

Their “wind-sled ” is 15m long and consists of four modules. It is propelled using 20 kites of various sizes. The largest kite measures 150m² and the smallest 5m². The four-man team plans a 3,400km, 85-day journey from near Novolazarevskaya Station to the Italian research station Concordia, known as Dome C, on the Antarctic Plateau.

The wind-sled team’s proposed route. The green return route is planned for a future expedition. Photo: La Vanguardia

Meanwhile, three Argentine kayakers are preparing to paddle roughly 900km along the western coast of the Antarctic Peninsula, from Argentina’s Esperanza Base to San Martín Base. Diego Linares, Juan Tomaselli, and Javier Siede will start their journey in January and plan to spend 30 to 40 days on the water.

Martin Walsh is a writer and editor for ExplorersWeb.

Martin spent most of the last 20 years backpacking the world on a shoestring budget. Whether it was hitchhiking through Syria, getting strangled in Kyrgyzstan, touring Cambodia’s medical facilities with an exceedingly painful giant venomous centipede bite, chewing khat in Ethiopia, or narrowly avoiding various toilet-related accidents in rural China, so far, Martin has just about survived his decision-making.

Based in Da Lat, Vietnam, Martin can be found in the jungle trying to avoid leeches while chasing monkeys.

EU taps industry to set standards for tokenized securities

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Applications close Nov. 27 for an industry group that aims to draft initial standards for holding and settling tokenized securities by early 2028.

The European Central Bank, European Commission and European Securities and Markets Authority are seeking nominations for an industry-led group that will draft common standards for holding and settling tokenized securities.

Eligible applicants include EU industry associations, global institutions, private firms and fintech companies. They can apply to the European Tokenisation Standards Group (ETSG) by Nov. 27, with work set to begin in January 2027, according to a release published Monday. The three EU institutions will participate as observers.

The group will develop technical and market standards to help companies hold and settle tokenized securities across different distributed ledger technology (DLT) platforms and ensure those platforms can work with existing financial systems. The group aims to produce an initial set of standards by early 2028, according to its mandate. The standards will support Appia, the Eurosystem’s initiative to develop a blueprint for Europe’s tokenized financial markets.

The institutions warned that fragmentation could limit the benefits of tokenization and said the group may recommend ways for EU authorities to encourage firms to adopt its standards.

In September, the Eurosystem launched Pontes, a system that allows financial institutions to settle wholesale transactions in tokenized assets in central bank money.

North Korea Is Minting Its Own Meme Coins to Launder Binance Hack Loot

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Elliptic says North Korean launderers created six throwaway meme tokens on NEAR between Nov. 5 and 7, moving about $430,000 of stolen Binance funds through homemade liquidity pools.

North Korean launderers are creating their own worthless meme coins and pushing stolen Binance funds through them so the coins never move directly from one wallet to another, blockchain analytics firm Elliptic said Monday.

Between Nov. 5 and 7, the operators minted at least six new tokens on the NEAR blockchain — named FUK, OLCCL, MEILI, MMLI, DABI and SINU — paired each with NEAR in its own liquidity pool, then bought the tokens with stolen NEAR and sold them back out through a second set of wallets. About 117,000 NEAR, or roughly $430,000, passed through the six tokens, Elliptic wrote.

The trail sits inside the broader laundering of the Oct. 29 Binance theft, which the exchange has put at $6.34 billion and which the FBI has attributed to North Korea’s TraderTraitor group. Elliptic’s wider update now puts roughly 3,000 BTC — about $225 million — in new bitcoin addresses converted from other stolen assets, while about 11.8 million NEAR, or $43.7 million, from the theft has still not moved.

“Most people think of meme tokens as joke coins whose prices rise and fall on hype,” Elliptic co-founder and chief scientist Dr. Tom Robinson wrote. “North Korea’s launderers have found a different use for them.”

The technique is a four-step hop. The North Korea launderers create a token with a public launch tool, open a liquidity pool pairing it with a real asset such as NEAR or a stablecoin, buy the new token with stolen funds from one set of wallets, then sell it back into the same pool from a second set of wallets that collect the real asset.

At no point do the stolen funds transfer directly from a buying wallet to a collecting wallet, because the pool sits in between. To a casual observer, the collecting wallets look like ordinary traders who sold a token at a profit.

Each of the six NEAR tokens cost about 25 NEAR, or around $90, to create. Each was traded heavily within hours using stolen NEAR and then abandoned. None has traded since Nov. 8, and that afternoon the creators of four of them withdrew what liquidity was left. Five of the six token creators were funded by a wallet that had just sold an earlier token, between two and 13 minutes before the next launch, so the tokens form a chain.

Elliptic first reported the same approach in the laundering of the February 2025 Bybit theft, also attributed to North Korean group TraderTraitor, when a worthless Tron token helped move at least $24 million. In the Binance case the firm has also seen the technique on Tron and a close variant on Solana that used existing thinly traded tokens rather than homemade ones.

The meme coins do not hide the funds. Every step is public on-chain. What they buy is time. Each extra hop slows investigators while launderers race to move funds before they are flagged, and at about $90 apiece the tokens are cheap enough to throw away.

That fits the larger pattern Elliptic has tracked since the theft: more than 20,000 wallets on 16 blockchains, at least 36 services and at least 65,000 transactions. Binance has said the attack began with a fake job interview and ran for five months before any coins moved. Blockchain firms linked the breach to TraderTraitor within a day.

The conversion into bitcoin is the bigger dollar story. All of the stolen SOL has already been swapped, bridged or mixed, and much of the XRP route has fed into bitcoin as well. About $2.85 billion of the original haul has been frozen. A negligible amount has likely been cashed out so far, Elliptic said.

North Korea’s crypto theft machine is not new. Elliptic put the North Korean regime’s 2025 haul above $2 billion, driven by the $1.46 billion Bybit exploit, and its cumulative known total above $6 billion even before Binance.

Binance has reopened withdrawals in stages and said it covered customer losses from its own funds. The test for investigators is whether homemade liquidity pools keep multiplying faster than the blocklists catching up to them.

I witnessed recurring Celtic Remembrance Sunday embarrassment but Green Brigade act commended

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Despite some rocky results, Celtic have made it to another international break still in front of Rangers. They are four points clear of the second-placed Ibrox side, who have a game in hand, after a narrow 1-0 Remembrance Sunday win over Kilmarnock.

Parkhead is unlike most places in the United Kingdom on Remembrance Sunday. Before kick-off, as fans milled around outside the stadium, there was barely a single poppy visible.

Did you know with a Digital Subscription to The Scotsman, you can get unlimited access to the website including our premium content, as well as benefiting from fewer ads, loyalty rewards and much more.

This was the 13th time since 1974, when Celtic first played a Sunday fixture, that they have been in action on Remembrance Sunday itself, the fifth time at home.

People wondered beforehand what would happen and whether a period of silence would be attempted at all. After all, when Celtic published their Kilmarnock matchday guide, including “everything you need to know about the game”, there was no mention of the day’s significance. All it advised was that supporters should be in their seat for the 4pm kick-off – put back due to Remembrance Sunday activities in Glasgow - to “make some noise for the Bhoys”. Well, many certainly managed this.

The Tannoy announcer stated there would be “some moments” of silence to commemorate the war dead. The Green Brigade opted to stay outside, which is to be commended. They clearly took the view that if others wished to take part in the tribute, then they could.

However, so many elsewhere in the stadium reacted by booing and jeering once John Beaton had blown his whistle to signal the start of the tribute that the referee swiftly ended. The ‘tribute’ was timed at just 13 seconds, which is some way short of the minute that is the norm on such occasions.

To be fair, the announcer had said the plan was for a "few moments’ silence" rather than a full minute. But there was barely even a few moments’ worth.

One wonders if Beaton had been given a recommendation by the authorities to wrap things up as quickly as possible if the booing started, because he did so almost immediately. Although it was an ugly cacophony, maybe it might have been better if he had just stood his ground and let it play out.

Others might argue it would have been better if the silence had not been scheduled – or attempted – at all. Everyone knew what was going to happen.

But then what about the Celtic fans who do wish to pay their respects – and there were plenty. And the Kilmarnock fans, small group though they were, had every right to expect to be able to stand and remember relatives who had made the greatest sacrifice.

The Green Brigade stayed away, which was fair enough. But many inside the ground, according to testimonies on Facebook and elsewhere, explained that they had wanted to observe a silence. The Celtic board have enough on their plate without seeking more aggravation. They are probably just happy that it's all over for another year. However, the vexed question of war remembrance is a recurring issue that embarrasses the club annually.

The issue of Kasper Hogh’s Parkhead goal drought dominated the post-match debate as did Mika Baur’s worrying ankle injury. So focus was not necessarily on the Parkhead return of Luke McCowan, whom many once wondered if they might ever see in a Celtic shirt again.

He was sent on in the dying minutes against Falkirk last weekend when Celtic were in dire straits. The stakes were higher this time. He replaced Benjamin Nygren with eight minutes left, and with Celtic desperate to hold on to a 1-0 lead. It was his first home appearance for Celtic since being taken off at half-time against Rangers on 20 September. You just can’t keep a good (Celtic) man down.

Some questioned whether he could ever recover from his disastrous cameo appearance against LASK in August as Celtic tumbled out of Champions League qualifying at the hands of their Austrian opponents.

After a few weeks in purdah, he started the league clash against Rangers just before the last international break. It didn’t go well. The midfielder was replaced at half time with Celtic trailing 1-0.

Might this stand as McCowan’s last appearance? Again, many assumed so. Tony Docherty, his former Dundee manager and now in charge of the Scotland Under-21s, recently mentioned on a podcast that the player was “having a tough time” having been bombed out of the squad completely.

However, the boyhood Celtic fan isn’t giving up on his Celtic career, that’s clear. Just as Hogh received warm applause when he left the field after 70 minutes, McCowan was greeted with similar when he was announced as coming on.

Admittedly, you feared for him slightly. Celtic were still hanging on to a 1-0 lead and it was getting increasingly nervy. One misplaced pass could have had dire consequence. But he was tidy and busy, and he even saw an effort slip through Nico Mantl’s fingers and into the net, although the whistle had already blown for a foul. Fans cheered, possibly slightly cruelly.

Who knows what it means for his Celtic future? Martin O’Neill didn’t broach the subject of McCowan specifically afterwards but he was asked about an unusual looking squad, which featured just one- goalkeeper Sam Johnstone - of the six late summer transfer deadline day acquisitions. Haissem Hassan, meanwhile, was a glaring omission. On this subject, O’Neill explained, slightly mysteriously, that it was all to do with squad “manoeuvring”.

The Clever Way to Reuse Old Tomato Cages to Protect Your Plants From Freezing Temperatures

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Beautiful plants and ideas to brighten your garden.

Expert tips for growing fruit, vegetables and herbs at home.

Sign up to get fresh gardening ideas, inspiration and expert advice delivered straight to your inbox.

What Your Hydrangeas Need in November – 5 Ways to Get Plants Winter-Ready and Kickstart Your Best Blooms Ever

These 7 Plants Need Pruning Before Winter – November is Your Last Chance to Make the Cut

My Husband Is a Professional Gardener – and He Always Does These 3 Things to Roses in November

Sign up for the Gardening Know How newsletter today and receive a free copy of our e-book "How to Grow Delicious Tomatoes".

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Your empty tomato cages can shelter tender shrubs from frost and snow all winter and help safeguard next summer's flowers.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Wrapped in frost cloth or burlap, an old tomato cage becomes a cheap but effective shelter to protect plants this winter. All sorts of tender shrubs and perennials need winter protection, and temporarily shielding even hardy plants from the worst of the weather can help them thrive. So if you're about to pack away your tomato cages in the shed, stop – they've got another job to do now it's time to winterize garden plants this fall.

Pulling up tomato plants and stacking their cages in the shed until spring is one of the last big gardening jobs of the season. That's a lot of good wire sitting around in the dark all winter, and this trick puts it right back to work. Most gardens have a plant or two that struggles through the cold months, and overwintering plants usually means spending money on shrub covers, frost tents or frames, or insulating zip-up plant jackets built for the job. None of it costs much alone, but the outlay adds up across a whole bed and your old cage can do much of that same work to winterize plants.

This trick won't save everything, but for the right plants a tomato cage wrapped in frost cloth or burlap is a smart, cheap way to help your garden get through winter unscathed. So, if you're gardening on a budget and wondering how to protect plants in winter, here's the smart solution.

Throw insulating fabric straight over a shrub and it settles right onto the leaves and buds, and cold will seep through wherever it touches. Wet snow is worse. Its weight can bend or snap branches under the cover. But put a tomato cage in there first and the fabric sits on the frame instead, with a little gap between cover and plant. This method also gives you a frame to fasten the fabric to so it stays put when the wind picks up.

Start with the cage itself. You'll probably find dried leaves and sections of vine still wound through the wire. If last summer's plants had Septoria leaf spot (Septoria lycopersici) or early blight (Alternaria solani), those scraps can carry it over, so pull every one out and disinfect by scrubbing the cage with diluted household bleach and a rinse.

Next comes the fit. Lower the clean cage over the plant and see where the branches end up. Any branch poking through the wire is, more or less, where the fabric will touch the plant later, so the stems need room inside. Push the legs down firmly and, out in an open, windy bed, tie it to a sturdy stake if needs be.

Now wrap the frame in breathable frost cloth or horticultural fleece: one Agfabric frost blanket, available from Home Depot, covers more than one cage. Fasten the fabric to the frame using clips like these stainless steel garden clips from Walmart. Take the fabric right down to the soil as warmth comes up out of the ground at night and a gap at the bottom lets it leak out. Pin the fabric to the ground with galvanized landscape staples like these from Walmart.

Sign up for the Gardening Know How newsletter today and receive a free copy of our e-book "How to Grow Delicious Tomatoes".

Skip plastic wrap, because the plant needs to breathe and a wrapped cage heats up fast on mild afternoons and damp, still air favors gray mold (Botrytis cinerea). Even without a waterproof wrap, peek underneath every few days and let some fresh air in if needs be. Once the cold snap is over, take the temporary covers off, leaving the frame in place ready for the next weather dip.

Depending on your USDA zone, the plants that need winterizing in fall aren't just those that aren't hardy enough to survive plummeting temperatures: you can use this trick to extend a crop or protect next year's flower show, too. Ever had a hydrangea leaf out nicely and then barely bloom? Bigleaf hydrangea (Hydrangea macrophylla) is the classic candidate because it forms next year's flower buds on old wood, and those buds sit on bare stems all winter. Hard freezes can kill these delicate buds so while the foliage comes through just fine the following year, blooms are few and far between.

Hybrid tea roses and perennials that are borderline hardy in your zone are good candidates too. If you've planted young shrubs this year – especially if you've been tempted by Black Friday sales on evergreen shrubs and you're planting late in fall – they'll also benefit from being kept cozy during cold snaps. Boxwood and other small evergreens can pick up winter burn from drying wind and sun, and burlap like this Wellco natural burlap roll at Home Depotcuts down on both without pressing on the foliage.

Of course, you'll need to match the plant to the cage size. But if you're planning to upgrade your tomato cages to use a more substantial modular tomato support set-up like these heavy-duty square tomato cages, also from Home Depot anyway, it makes sense to do so now when you can use them to protect bigger shrubs that need a wider frame.

Once the plant goes dormant, usually after several hard frosts, a sturdy cage can be packed with loose insulation to provide further insulation too. Dry shredded leaves, straw or pine straw like this from Amazon is best. On a bigleaf hydrangea, you can fill right up around the buds, while roses mostly need the extra insulation over the crown and lower canes.

Keep that fill dry and loose. Leaves raked off a wet lawn and packed tight against living foliage or a crown stay soggy, and that can lead to rot, so let them dry out first before covering with frost fabric. In spring, pull out the fill a little at a time once the worst cold has passed.

Frost cloth doesn't make any heat of its own, but only slows the loss of warmth rising from the ground at night, buying a few degrees on a clear, still night. In a long, deep freeze, the soil underneath cools off too, and that leaves the cover less and less to work with. Truly tropical plants won't turn winter-hardy inside a wrapped cage, either: hibiscus and mandevilla belong indoors once nights turn chilly, well ahead of frost. In colder regions, some tender plants on the edge of hardiness still do better potted up and stored in a frost-free spot.

For most of the year, a tomato cage has one job. But with a cozy comforter of frost cloth, a few clips and a bit of care, this garden staple picks up a second role in winter, sheltering plants that would otherwise need a store-bought cover and helping to ensure another flower-filled year in your garden. When spring comes, unwrap your precious plant, hose off the cage and send it back to the tomato bed. It's the same old cage pulling double duty, and this trick to reuse it over winter barely costs a dime.

Tyler’s passion began with indoor gardening and deepened as he studied plant-fungi interactions in controlled settings. With a microbiology background focused on fungi, he’s spent over a decade solving tough and intricate gardening problems. After spinal injuries and brain surgery, Tyler’s approach to gardening changed. It became less about the hobby and more about recovery and adapting to physical limits. His growing success shows that disability doesn’t have to stop you from your goals.

Atmos Rewards Credit Card Bonus Offers & Eligibility Rules Explained

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Atmos Rewards is the loyalty program of Alaska Airlines and Hawaiian Airlines, and the program has three co-branded credit cards in the United States — the Atmos™ Rewards Summit Visa Infinite® Credit Card, Atmos™ Rewards Ascent Visa Signature® credit card, and Atmos™ Rewards Visa Signature® Business Card — all of which are excellent.

In this post, I’d like to go over the details of the welcome offers on these cards. After that, I’ll talk a bit about card eligibility. Let me say up front that it’s possible to get more than one of these cards, so you’re probably eligible for one of these even if you already have a card in the portfolio.

Link: Learn about the best credit cards for Alaska Atmos Rewards

Bank of America and Alaska Air Group partner on issuing three co-branded credit cards, all with Atmos Rewards branding, replacing the previous Mileage Plan program. Let’s cover the details of the bonuses, plus the basics of why you may want to consider these cards.

The Atmos Rewards Summit Card has a $395 annual fee, and is the most premium personal card in the collection.

The card is offering a limited time welcome bonus where you can receive 100,000 bonus points and a 25,000-point Global Companion Award with this offer. To qualify, spend $6,000 or more on purchases within the first 90 days of opening your account.

While this card has the highest annual fee of the three cards, it offers an amazing value proposition. Perks include the following, among other things:

Read a detailed review of the Atmos Rewards Summit Card.

The Atmos Rewards Ascent Card has a $95 annual fee, and is the more basic of the two personal cards in the collection.

The card is offering a limited time welcome bonus where you can receive 60,000 bonus points and a $99 Companion Fare (plus taxes and fees from $23) after spending $3,000 or more on purchases within the first 90 days of opening your account. You can earn an additional 20,000 bonus points after spending a total of $5,000 or more on purchases within the same 90-day period.

This is a great option for a low-fee card that still offers considerable value for someone who flies Alaska or Hawaiian with any frequency. Some of the card’s perks include the following:

Read a detailed review of the Atmos Rewards Ascent Card.

The Atmos Rewards Business Card has a minimum of a $95 annual fee ($70 for the account and $25 per card), and is the business card in the portfolio.

The card is offering a welcome bonus of 70,000 bonus points and a $99 Companion Fare (plus taxes and fees from $23) after you make $4,000 or more in purchases within the first 90 days of opening your account.

The perks on this card are very similar to the perks on its personal card equivalent. These include the following, among other things:

Read a detailed review of the Atmos Rewards Business Card.

With the above out of the way, who is eligible for the welcome offers on the Atmos Rewards Summit Card, Atmos Rewards Ascent Card, and Atmos Rewards Business Card?

Let me start by stating that eligibility for all three Atmos Rewards cards (including the welcome offers) is considered independently. So if you already have the Atmos Rewards Ascent Card, you can apply for the Atmos Rewards Summit Card, and vice versa. The same is true for the Atmos Rewards Business Card. So rules can’t get easier in that regard.

How does card eligibility work if you have an existing Alaska Airlines Bank of America card, or Hawaiian Airlines Barclays card?

Let’s start with the easier part of the answer. If you have or have had either of the Hawaiian Airlines Barclays cards (whether personal or business) that doesn’t impact your ability to qualify for any of the Atmos Rewards cards, including the welcome bonuses.

Meanwhile if you have any former Alaska Airlines Bank of America cards, those cards have been converted (or are in the process of being converted) into one of these new Atmos Rewards cards:

So you may not be eligible for the bonus on one of those cards if you currently have it. However, you’d likely be eligible for the bonus on whichever card you don’t have.

What about if you’ve had one of the above cards in the past? Bank of America doesn’t generally have a “once in a lifetime” rule. Instead, you typically have to wait 24 months from when you closed a card before you can qualify for the bonus on a card again (though some report earning a bonus sooner than that).

However, that’s not a published rule, and you’ll find that offer terms call this a “one-time promotion” that’s “limited to customers opening a new account.”

Beyond the rules specific to the Atmos Rewards cards, what general things should you be aware of if applying for a Bank of America card? Here are the two most common Bank of America restrictions:

Neither of those rules seem to be totally consistently enforced, and in particular, that latter policy doesn’t seem consistent at all. For example, when I applied for Atmos Rewards cards last year, I had opened more than three new cards in the previous 12 months, and was still approved.

How hard are Bank of America cards to get approved for, in general? Obviously this is only based on anecdotal reports, but in my experience, Bank of America cards are generally quite easy to get approved for, and I also find they typically have quite large credit limits. However, the issuer can also be quirky with denials, especially based on the above two rules.

There’s no published credit score you need to get approved for Atmos Rewards credit cards, as it’s one of only many factors that impacts your approval odds. I’d hope to have a score in the “good” to “excellent” range — ideally I’d have a score of 700+ points, but people with lower scores have been approved, and people with higher scores have been rejected.

What’s the best play if you’re considering picking up an Atmos Rewards credit card, between the Atmos Rewards Summit Card, Atmos Rewards Ascent Card, and Atmos Rewards Business Card? Honestly, you can’t go wrong with any combination of cards here. A few thoughts:

For example, I’ve shared my experience getting approved for the Atmos Rewards Summit Card, and also my experience getting approved for the Atmos Rewards Business Card.

There are three Atmos Rewards credit cards, including the premium Atmos Rewards Summit Card, plus the Atmos Rewards Ascent Card and Atmos Rewards Business Card.

These are all worthwhile cards, so if you’re eligible for any of these cards, they’re worth considering. One thing that’s great is that eligibility requirements for these cards aren’t too strict, in the sense that you can qualify for each of these cards, and earn the welcome offers. Hopefully the above is a useful rundown of the rules surrounding getting approved for these cards.

Are there any Atmos Rewards credit card application questions I haven’t covered? If you’ve applied for one of these cards, what was your experience like?

Consider this blackened plug your weekly reminder to check your RTX 50-series GPU cables

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

If you have a high-end RTX 50-series graphics card then kudos to you. You either got in there early and avoided the massive price hikes that followed, or you managed to get a second mortgage and pick one up anyways. But either way, you're going to want to protect your investment because replacements are hard and expensive to find. And it looks like you really need to protect that investment because there are still regular cases of melting 12+4 pin power cables.

This latest (via Uniko's Hardware) serves as a reminder that getting a cable monitoring solution is a good way to protect your expensive graphics card, as user Edistonian2 notes in a recent reddit thread on the ThermalGrizzly sub. They note that they picked up an RTX 5090 in June and subsequently grabbed a WireView Pro II monitor to keep it safe.

The WireView noted odd current behaviour with two of the pins, while HWInfo said it was fine and a Gemini chat suggested it was an issue with the monitor. I'm sure that's what Nvidia would say, too... So they replace their WireView with a new one, and still get the current issues on pin four. So they removed the cable entirely and the image up top is what was happening on the PSU end of the wire.

They've since picked up another cable and swapped to the other GPU power port on the GPU and so far everything is fine, but it just shows that there are still power issues going on with these cables and a monitoring system can be a worthwhile step in between finding your power leads or plugs melting, or your GPU going up in a puff of smoke.

Now, it's not the cheapest method—the WireView Pro II is $190 at Amazon right now—but that is chump change in relation to the ~$8,000 you might have to spend on a replacement RTX 5090. There are also cheaper alternatives, such as Cooler Master's GPU Shield, which is $50, and the Super Flower 12+4 pin OCP Dongle.

Whichever you choose, as the pricing crisis continues for high-end cards, that's going to be a small price to pay to save something catastrophic happening to your expensive silicon.

2. Best value: AMD Radeon RX 9060 XT 16 GB

5. Best high-end: Nvidia GeForce RTX 5090

Man Utd: Carrick Future Uncertain With Howe and Conte on Manager Shortlist

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Michael Carrick’s position at Manchester United remains uncertain following the 4-4 draw against Aston Villa on Sunday, GIVEMESPORT understands.

Man United considered sacking Carrick after they lost 4-1 to Como and 3-0 at home to Bournemouth in successive games, before deciding to give him until the international break to show an upturn in form.

United have since lost 2-0 at Chelsea in the Premier League, before they came from 2-0 down to beat Roma 3-2 in the Champions League on Tuesday, in what Carrick would have been hoping was a turning point.

The Red Devils got off to the worst possible start when they hosted Aston Villa at Old Trafford on Sunday, finding themselves 2-0 down after four minutes, and then 4-2 down after 53 minutes, but they twice reacted well to salvage a point in the dying seconds of the game.

Still, United have won just one Premier League match all season and lie in the relegation zone, with further boardroom talks now expected before they play again against Liverpool at Anfield a week on Sunday.

It has been calm at Carrington since the draw at Villa, but talks will take place to discuss the future of Carrick.

Should club chiefs decide that a change in the dugout doesn't need to be made, then GMS understands that one option is to add to the 45-year-old's coaching staff.

Aaron Danks is appreciated by the club, but he is also unlikely to leave Bayern Munich mid-season.

Danks worked under Carrick at Middlesbrough and is currently in charge of Bayern while Vincent Kompany recovers after collapsing, while he also had a two-game spell in caretaker charge of Aston Villa previously.

GMS also understands that United are unlikely to appoint another interim manager if they do decide to make a change during the international break, despite the success of Carrick in that role last term.

Eddie Howe is one of the leading contenders, with the out-of-work manager available to start the role immediately having decided to part ways with Newcastle United in the summer.

Julian Nagelsmann is also appreciated by people at the club and, like Howe, is available after leaving his role as Germany manager in the summer.

Meanwhile, Unai Emery looks set to sign a new deal at Aston Villa having had his admirers on the United board, while GMS understands Brighton boss Fabian Hurzeler doesn't want to leave, and his contract has no release clause.

And former Tottenham manager Mauricio Pochettino was considered prior to Ruben Amorim’s appointment, but has since signed a new deal with the USMNT.

Other options include Antonio Conte and Simone Inzaghi, who is the current head coach of Saudi Pro League club Al-Hilal.

Man United will only need to make that decision if they choose to sack Carrick, with the upcoming talks during the break crucial to what happens next.

The Red Devils' boss is feeling the pressure as United head into the international break in the relegation zone.

Lineker: Leeds Star Tarik Muharemovic a Contender for Signing of the Season

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Leeds United ended a run of five Premier League matches without a win by beating Tottenham Hotspur 1-0 on Saturday, in a game that led to Gary Lineker describing one of their players as a potential signing of the season.

Noah Okafor's late winner ensured Leeds moved back into the top half of the table, and while it wasn't a classic top flight encounter, Daniel Farke's side did enough to secure the three points against an in-form Spurs side.

The Yorkshire outfit's struggles in front of goal looked like they were going to continue on the weekend prior to Okafor's goal, which was their 10th in 10 Premier League matches this term.

While there are still concerns about Farke's side in the final third, what is clear is that they are defensively sound.

Only the top two in the league, Arsenal and Brighton and Hove Albion, have conceded fewer goals than Leeds (8), and it is a record to be proud of.

There is no doubt that the club have been boosted significantly from a defensive point of view by the club-record addition of goalkeeper James Trafford, whose stunning save from Jan Paul van Hecke in the dying stages on Saturday preserved the three points.

Leeds have looked defensively solid in Farke's preferred 3-5-2 formation, with Jayden Bogle and Gabriel Gudmundsson impressing as wing-backs, and the first-choice defensive trio of James Justin, Nico Elvedi and Tarik Muharemovic impressing too.

The latter joined from Sassuolo in the summer in a deal worth £34m, and so far he is looking to be worth every single penny.

That is the view of Lineker, speaking on the latest episode of the Rest is Football podcast.

Speaking about the potential player of the season contenders in the Premier League, the pundit said:

I just want to say Ezri Konza, possibly signing of the season. If not, Muharemovic at Leeds has done really well.

"[Nicolas] Jackson, of course, banging them in. Elliot Anderson, even though they've lost the last three, has been terrific. Morgan Rogers, [Jeremy] Jacquet at Liverpool looks impressive, or even the Villa goalkeeper [Zion] Suzuki."

The Bosnia and Herzegovina promised when he signed that he loved defending and would give everything in a Leeds shirt, and he has lived up to that so far.

The 23-year-old has been willing to throw his body on the line to help the Yorkshire outfit pick up points, and is working well in a back three under Farke.

Muharemovic was brought to the club to help with their defensive record, but he is also making an impact in the final third for the Whites too.

You would expect a 6ft 4in centre-back to potentially provide goals from set pieces, but he instead has picked up two assists for the club in his 10 Premier League appearances, against Newcastle United and Manchester United.

No Leeds player has assisted more in the top flight this term, so he is proving to be influential from an attacking sense too, as the Yorkshire outfit look to increase their goal contribution numbers.

The fact that someone like Lineker has noticed how good he has been and is naming him as a potential signing of the season contender tells you all you need to know about how good he has been.

Farke will hope he continues at this level, because if the defence stays as good as it is right now, and they can add more goals in the coming months, a top half finish is very much on the cards.

Leeds United have a new hero in town after the Whites recorded a much-needed 1-0 win against Tottenham

It feels like only yesterday that Halo: Campaign Evolved teased a remake of Halo 2, something I've been hoping for more and more as time went on. After all, it's been 22 long years now. Yep, that's right - Halo 2 launched on November 9, 2004 for the original Xbox, and has often been credited as the game that put Xbox Live on the map.

As the years slip by, anniversaries for OG Xbox titles have a habit of making me feel my age. Still, what a privilege it was to be there for the inception of Xbox Live, to play both co-op and PvP with friends across the globe in the second entry of Bungie's incredible run of Halo games.

Admittedly, it's been a hot-minute since I last booted up Halo 2 in the Master Chief Collection. But visits to retro gaming bars like Manchester's NQ64 reminded me just how good this FPS still is. I recall sitting down for a few games of Slayer, only for the muscle memory to kick in and humble a good number of Hookshot staff - Fraser included. Oh boy, it never gets old.

In fact, just sit back and listen to 15 minutes of Halo 2's main menu music and ponder the good old days of dual-wielding SMGs in Beaver Creek.

Halo 2 took Master Chief's fight against the Covenant to Earth, selling more than 8.4 million copies before its servers shut down in 2010, following the discontinuation of the original Xbox Live services. The game was later ported to PC and remastered for Xbox One as part of Halo: The Master Chief Collection.

Xbox's 25th anniversary is just round the corner: in fact, it's this Sunday! We'll be here, keeping an eye out for any and all announcements from Xbox throughout the week. Understandably, we've been feeling pretty nostalgic, hence this Halo 2 feature. In fact, I'm reinstalling the MCC as we speak.

I was a big fan of Halo Studios' Campaign Evolved. Like, I really, really enjoyed it. So, it's bittersweet knowing that their teased Halo 2 remake in Unreal Engine will never happen.

Sure, the devs fumbled modern Halo multiplayer, but perhaps a Halo 2 remake could have been a case of history repeating itself. Y'know, a remade return of Halo 2's iconic online multiplayer?! Ah, well.

Apple staff accused of signing up customers to subscriptions without consent; company responds

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Repair technician turned consumer rights advocate Louis Rossmann recently accused Apple store staff of signing up customers to the company’s services subscriptions without their consent.

Apple has not yet commented, but has taken two steps indicating that the company takes the accusations seriously …

Rossmann is a repair technician who specializes in component-level logic board repairs and data recovery. He later became a key figure in advocating for right-to-repair legislation, which Apple initially opposed and later backed.

In a recent video, he claimed that some Apple store staff were signing up customers to trials of paid services subscriptions without their informed consent. Targets set by Apple are said to have encouraged the practice.

Customers accepting the offer of a personalized shopping experience could be shown the free trials of Apple services available to them based on their recent purchases. For example, buying an iPhone currently entitles you to a free three-month subscription to Apple TV. However, these trials automatically start debiting your payment method if you don’t cancel them before they end.

Staff were instructed to show these offers to customers and were allegedly set a 40% sign-up target, which Apple tracked as a performance metric. Crucially, the offers are shown on the employee’s device rather than on the customer’s phone. A store member told Rossman that some staff were hitting the Accept button without properly obtaining consent from customers in order to hit their target.

When I asked whether they’d seen a specialist press Agree with their own eyes, they answered “Yes, I saw some specialists press on behalf of the customer. So, they are hitting agree. They are signing the customer up to something without actually having the customer click okay, hit okay, or provide express consent.”

The store member also claimed that staff were describing the offer as a gift, which further disguised the fact that it would turn into a paid recurring subscription unless canceled.

This is not shocking to me. Apple started pushing subscriptions during the sales process years ago when I worked in the stores.

I remember this being a major shift from being more customer focused over to becoming a battleground of a sales floor trying to get your "numbers". I even recall hearing that someone got promoted because their numbers were "so good" while we all knew they were signing people for AC+ without their knowledge. Sad days.

At the time of writing, Apple has not responded directly to the accusations, but in a follow-up video yesterday Rossman cites a staff member describing the company’s response.

He says a memo was read out in the morning meeting stating that only customers could press the Accept button, and internal systems indicate Apple is no longer grading staff on the signup metric.

We have reached out to Apple for comment and will update with any response.

The key question now is whether this emerging strength can sustain. Can Bitcoin Cash maintain its momentum and reclaim higher levels in 2026? Here’s a detailed breakdown of 2026-2030-year trajectory.

BCH coin has spent considerable time building a base, absorbing supply and stabilizing after previous volatility. In this scenario, BCH could advance toward the $600–$1200 range in 2026, reflecting a full expansion cycle driven by structural breakout. However, if momentum weakens and key supports fail, the market may revert to a range-bound phase before attempting another move higher.

MSI Maestro 900R PC case review

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

You will get a super-clean final build out of the Maestro 900R, and it will look stunning for the most part. But when you get up close, there are some parts which don't feel totally premium, and the price is as much of a barrier as the tempered glass side panels are to actually getting to your system's I/O ports.

PC Gamer's got your back Our experienced team dedicates many hours to every review, to really get to the heart of what matters most to you. Find out more about how we evaluate games and hardware.

It's just me in the office today. Normally that's fine. I don't mind the quiet, and I can be as messy as I like without Jacob tutting at me. Today is different. Today that comes with a healthy dose of fear. Fear because I have to lift the MSI Maestro 900R case up onto the test bench desk, and I don't think Paul, the oddball security guard way downstairs at the front desk, will hear me if I scream because my spine has snapped. It's a two person job and I'm very much a one-person team in PC Gamer towers today. I have managed to get it situated on the desk, but I can certainly feel it in my near fifty-year-old back muscles. *Spasm*. And when you need another human on hand every time you want to shift your gaming PC about, I think that says a lot about both this MSI case and who it's aimed at.

In short, I think you need to be someone who has their own staff.

That's partly a dig at its sheer scale—this thing is chomk with a capital CHOMK—but also at the fact only the very wealthy are going to consider this chassis a worthwhile casing for their gaming PC. This is a $700 (£800) PC case and that feels, on one hand, par for the course in these days of hyper-expensive everything, but also faintly anachronistic.

As always with the case reviewing process, I built a new PC inside it, and it only felt right to be doing so with high-end gear. So, I picked from the review kit that was in the cupboard: a MSI Godlike X with a Ryzen 9 9950X3D seemed appropriate, along with some 64 GB of G.Skill ULL memory and an RTX 5090. And that's when this whole endeavour started to feel like something from a bygone age.

Before, when I'd do a high-end build, it felt exciting in a way that was both special—because I could never spend out on such premium parts myself—but also still kind of achievable, too, because the money wasn't so far beyond the realms of decency that you couldn't imagine someone normal pushing their budget a little far to get this sort of kit. But looking at the parts going into this, it's around $12,000 worth of kit at least. And that feels well beyond a normal PC gamer and into faintly distasteful territory.

But, in all honesty, I'm not even totally convinced the MSI Maestro 900R really deserves its place in such company. Sure, it's elegant in black and gold trim, but there are odd touches here and there which belie the super premium pricing. The most egregious is something I picked out when I saw a preview case out at Computex this year, and that's the top flair on the case. The metal 'lid' only covers half of the dust filter for the 360 mm cooler I'd mounted to the top, and that filter is just a flimsy plastic one I wouldn't really want on display.

I also question the utility of having the motherboard and GPU's outputs all out of reach in the centre of the case, behind tempered glass shields. There are a ton of Type-A and Type-C USB ports on the front panel, but still it's a lot of effort to get to the I/O panel of your motherboard. And don't get me started on the PSU buried right in the back of the case, with impossible-to-reach switches and plug sockets.

The PC Gamer Circuit Breaker newsletter breaks down the latest PC gaming tech, and finds you the best deals on the best products

But there are things I like, too. The wraparound glass does look great, and I loved having so much space to work in when building into the rig. And to have that full central motherboard tray be completely removable was a treat, as well. You essentially have your own little test rig there, so you can make sure everything works before you do the full install. It's neat. As is the final build.

But honestly, this feels more like a chassis you might buy for someone else to build into. Because you're Mr. or Mrs. Moneybags and don't want to get your hands dirty doing manual labour and just want to have a high-end rig to play your games on in something that stands like a glass and gold monolith in the bespoke gaming room of your palace.

✅ You only have a passing knowledge of what's in your bank account: If you watch the pennies this isn't a chassis for you, but if you don't—and you're into the black, glass, and gold aesthetic, and have the space—then maybe it's worth considering.

❌ You need regular access to your mobo's I/O panel: The Maestro 900R hides it all away inside the case, meaning you have to open it up to get at the back of your motherboard.

You will get a super-clean final build out of the Maestro 900R, and it will look stunning for the most part. But when you get up close there are some parts which don't feel totally premium, and the price is as much of a barrier as the tempered glass side panels are to actually getting to your system's I/O ports.

Regular: E-ATX / ATX / Micro-ATX / Mini-ITX

1 x LED Switch Button · 1 x Mic-in / Audio-out · 1 x Power Button · 4 x USB 5Gbps (Type-A) · 4 x USB 10Gbps (Type-C) · 1 x USB 20Gbps (Type-C)

Top: 4 x 120 mm / 3 x 140 mm / 3 x 160 mm · Side: 6 x 120 mm · Bottom: 4 x 120 mm / 3 x 140 mm / 3 x 160 mm

Top: 120 / 140 / 240 / 280 / 360 / 420 mm · Side: 120 / 240 / 360 mm · Bottom: 120 / 140 / 240 / 280 / 360 / 420 mm

695 x 379 x 671 mm / 27.36 x 14.92 x 26.42 inches

Net weight: 33 kg / 72.75 lbs · Gross weight: 38 kg / 83.78 lbs

The key things to note are the sheer size of this beast. At well over 30 kilos, this is a massive chassis and when you've built a rig into it, that weight only goes up. It's a two-person job shifting this thing around, as my poor back can attest to.

The other thing is that it is really pleasing to build into. Thanks, in part, to the size of the case and the space you have to work in, but also because of that central motherboard tray. It is effectively a standalone test bench, and makes putting the board itself in place a doddle. It's also rotatable, so you can orient your system inside however you'd like. And because all the external cables need to be routed around from inside to outside anyways, you don't have to worry about where the mobo's I/O is going to go because it'll largely be inaccessible anyways.

To help this you have four 10 Gbps Type-C USB ports on the front of the case, along with a further 20 Gbps Type-C with 60 W power delivery, and four 5 Gbps USB Type-A ports. That does salve some of the pain of not being able to access your motherboard's back panel.

The other useful thing is that it comes with three 160 mm reverse blade fans, so the intakes on the base don't show their ugly side and ruin the high-end aesthetic.

Installation is an absolute joy… until it isn't. That occurs towards the end of the build, but at the start it really is all fun and games. The removable fan tray makes it easy to get your 360 mm radiator in place and the scale of the case means there's always space to work in when you've pulled the tool-free side panels off.

That motherboard tray is excellent, too. I was a big fan of that when I saw it out at Computex, and have been just as impressed now I've used it properly. The issue is that if you want to test out the components you've bought for this build using it as a standalone test bench, you will have to largely break it all down again before you can jam it into the system itself. But that's to be expected, and being able to just get your motherboard, CPU, cooling bracket, SSD, and RAM in place free from even the modest constraints of this massive rig is a real boon.

The installation pain is rear-loaded, however, and once you've got everything inside and wired up internally it then becomes a game of figuring out how the hell you're actually going to attach this thing up to either power or a monitor. There is no real back to this case, with the wraparound tempered glass surrounding the entire plane you mount the motherboard too. That means all the cables, from power, to display, to networking and anything else going into the back of your board, need to be routed out of some grommeted holes on the underside of the case.

It means you'll likely need a longer HDMI or DP cable than you have right now, because it needs to be routed in and around the inside of the case to get to the vertically mounted graphics card, which cuts a lot off its length. And you're unlikely to be sitting this on a desktop given its girth, so it's going to need to stretch from the ground up. Similarly, the power cable for the PSU has to be squeezed into the case, and if you're using a large PSU—such as the MSI MEG Ai1600T brick I've been using—then that doesn't give you a lot of space to attach its fat plug.

But that lack of a busy, cable-heavy backside to the case does really aid the aesthetic. The shroud (which is sadly quite flimsy plastic) that covers the rear of the vertically mounted GPU and the rear of the motherboard hides all the cables that sprout from their outputs as they're fed out the underside of the case, and that gives it a really clean finish. Inside, it looks like there is almost nothing connecting the components, leaving them looking like they're floating around inside. The MSI Godlike X motherboard only helped this by hiding a lot of its power cabling, too.

The angled reverse-bladed fans on the bottom intake are slightly angled, which allows you to see them in all their RGB LED glory, but that, coupled with the angling of the top radiator mount, makes it feel even bigger inside. I really like the full wraparound tempered glass, too. It really gives it a showpiece aesthetic, which I can see being used at a bunch of PC trade show booths in the future.

What slightly ruins that aesthetic, however, is the exposed plastic dust cover on the top of the case. The metal cover, which slots on without screws, only partially covers the things, leaving it open to be eyeballed. And it's not a great look. Likewise, the plastic shroud covering the cables inside looks and feels flimsy. It's the same with the power and LED buttons on the front panel. They look like they could have come from any $50 chassis, as does the lightbar on the rear side of the case's top.

It is not the most expensive PC chassis you can buy on Newegg right now. But that's mostly because Newegg's marketplace is an odd place to be and there are a ton of Hatsune Miku Asus cases on there with four-figure price tags, and I can't figure out why. There's also a $1,000 Asus ProArt chassis on there, but you can also just buy that for $300 direct from Newegg if you'd rather. Which is a roundabout way of me saying that it is pretty much the most expensive chassis you can buy on Newegg without delving into the murky realms of the marketplace.

And, personally, I find it hard to reconcile any notions of value against the MSI Maestro 900R beyond the sheer mass of the thing. Were we going by size and weight, then, I guess it's a match for the Asus ProArt PA602. That's half the weight and half the price, so on a purely per-gram metric, it's all evens Antoine Griezmanns.

But in real terms, it's a massive, expensive case, that I struggle to find genuine ways to justify its cost. There are some neat features, such as that mobo tray, but also some genuine points of frustration that would have me not actually wanting to clothe my PC parts in its black, gold, and glass surrounds. I'm happy with my—what seems to be rather diminutive now—Hyte Y70 Touch. Again, it's half the price, but at least I can get at my PC bits and not break my spine picking it up off the ground.

4. Best budget compact: Thermaltake S100 TG Snow Edition

You will get a super-clean final build out of the Maestro 900R, and it will look stunning for the most part. But when you get up close, there are some parts which don't feel totally premium, and the price is as much of a barrier as the tempered glass side panels are to actually getting to your system's I/O ports.

Dave has been gaming since the days of Zaxxon and Lady Bug on the Colecovision, and code books for the Commodore Vic 20 (Death Race 2000!). He built his first gaming PC at the tender age of 16, and finally finished bug-fixing the Cyrix-based system around a year later. When he dropped it out of the window. He first started writing for Official PlayStation Magazine and Xbox World many decades ago, then moved onto PC Format full-time, then PC Gamer, TechRadar, and T3 among others. Now he's back, writing about the nightmarish graphics card market, CPUs with more cores than sense, gaming laptops hotter than the sun, and SSDs more capacious than a Cybertruck.

Ref watch: Dermot Gallagher makes telling Brentford and Igor Thiago observation after red card at Brighton

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Former Premier League referee Dermot Gallagher believes Igor Thiago's red card in the 2-0 loss at Brighton was ”not a terrible decision.”

Brentford striker Thiago was initially shown a yellow card by referee Michael Oliver after reacting to a shirt pull and subsequent push from Albion midfielder Chema Andres, who was also booked.

Did you know with an ad-lite subscription to SussexWorld, you get 70% fewer ads while viewing the news that matters to you.

But the punishment was upgraded to red on review as match official Oliver deemed the Brazil international used “force” to push his opponent in the face with a raised arm.

Brentford were already trailing at the time of Thiago’s sending-off following a stunning long-range opener from Brighton midfielder Diego Gomez.

Pascal Gross doubled Albion’s lead with a 42nd-minute penalty awarded for Keane Lewis-Potter’s shirt pull on Luka Vuskovic.

Gallagher, speaking on Sky Sports Ref Watch, said: "I'll be saying to players, this is what referees are doing.

“This is how they're acting, why put yourself in this position? We've had three [incidents like this] over the last few weeks.

“I said, don't give the referee a decision today.”

Fellow Ref Watch pundit Jay Bothroyd then chipped in: “I'm sorry, first of all, the [Brighton] defender [Chema] has initiated that whole incident, right? He's caused that incident.

“When I look at that, to me, Thiago's just trying to strike him off. His arm has brushed up his shirt and hit him in the face.

“You can't give red cards for that. I'm sorry. You just can't. That is a terrible decision to make.”

Gallagher though backed Oliver as he was working within the rules. He responded: “It's a wrong decision. I'll accept that, a wrong decision.

“Not a terrible decision, because the referees have asked. I get that you think it's wrong, but it's not a terrible decision.”

Meanwhile, Brentford boss Keith Andrews questioned the use of VAR in the Premier League after the Thiago incident.

“I’m struggling to believe in this day and age where we see the referee’s call on pitch and the bar of where they’re given the licence to referee in-game, which is what I should think it should be, but he’s been sent to the screen,” he said.

“We play on the edge, we’re a team that likes to play on the edge, I want us to be on the edge. But it clearly cost us.”

Circle Internet Group Inc. (NYSE: CRCL) stock has entered a critical phase after falling nearly 44% from its $159.47 52-week high, but the long-term growth story remains intact. The pullback has brought CRCL stock back into focus as stablecoin adoption, USDC circulation and digital-asset payments continue to expand. With Circle building a broader financial infrastructure around USDC, investors are looking beyond the current correction toward its next growth cycle.

Can CRCL reclaim $150 and deliver stronger long-term returns as the stablecoin market scales? Here’s our Circle stock price prediction for 2026–2040.

Circle Internet Group is positioning itself beyond a single stablecoin product, building a broader digital financial infrastructure around USDC. Its growth strategy centers on expanding stablecoin adoption, payments, blockchain infrastructure and institutional use cases. With these businesses scaling alongside the wider shift toward on-chain finance, CRCL’s long-term valuation will depend on how effectively Circle converts USDC’s growing ecosystem into sustainable revenue and earnings growth.

Circle is increasingly being valued as a stablecoin and digital-financial infrastructure platform, rather than simply a USDC issuer. The core investment thesis rests on the continued expansion of USDC, rising transaction activity and Circle’s ability to capture greater economic value as stablecoins move deeper into payments, settlement and institutional finance. Q2 2026 showed USDC circulation at $73.3 billion, while transaction volume reached $14.8 trillion, highlighting the scale of the network already being built.

The next phase of growth will depend on monetization and ecosystem expansion. Circle Payments Network, Arc, institutional blockchain applications and additional financial products could diversify revenue beyond reserve income and increase the strategic value of Circle’s infrastructure. The company’s expanding institutional footprint also strengthens the case for USDC becoming a more embedded settlement layer across global finance.

For the long-term CRCL stock price prediction, the key metric is therefore not simply USDC circulation but Circle’s ability to convert that scale into sustainable revenue, operating leverage and higher-margin platform economics. If adoption continues accelerating while Circle successfully expands its ecosystem, the current valuation reset could provide the foundation for a stronger multi-year growth cycle

On the upside, reclaiming $80 would be the first meaningful sign of improving momentum, while the broader supply zone remains around $135–$145

CoinPedia expects that CRCL stock may reach $135-$140 by 2026, while maintaining support above $80 is crucial for the bullish structure to remain intact.

Circle reported better-than-expected third-quarter 2026 results on November 4, with earnings of $0.84 per share, compared with analysts’ expectations of $0.38. Revenue and reserve income reached approximately $865 million, up 17% year over year, while average USDC circulation increased 8% to $73.3 billion.

However, the earnings beat came with some underlying concerns. Reserve income declined 8% to $658 million as lower reserve yields weighed on the business. Circle also disclosed a workforce reduction of approximately 5%, while $182 million in ARC token presale revenue contributed significantly to other revenue. These factors have raised questions about the sustainability of earnings growth.

Circle Internet Group’s long-term valuation is increasingly tied to whether it can turn USDC adoption into a broader payments and financial infrastructure business. The stock remains highly sensitive to stablecoin circulation and reserve economics, but the next phase of growth will depend on transaction activity, subscription and services revenue, institutional adoption and Circle’s ability to expand beyond crypto trading.

Our analysis focuses on the factors most directly connected to Circle’s future earnings potential, including USDC circulation growth, reserve income, subscription and services revenue, payments activity, institutional adoption, and expansion of stablecoin infrastructure. The pace at which Circle converts growing USDC usage into diversified, recurring revenue is central to the long-term CRCL investment thesis.

CRCL’s valuation is assessed against Circle’s position in the rapidly developing stablecoin and digital-payments industry. The final CRCL stock price prediction weighs these factors together to establish bearish, base-case and bullish valuation paths. CRCL is moving into a phase where USDC scale, revenue diversification and operating leverage could determine the stock’s next major valuation cycle. For the CRCL stock price prediction through 2030 and beyond, the decisive factor is execution. Sustained USDC adoption combined with improving revenue quality could shift CRCL from a high-growth stablecoin proxy into a broader digital-financial infrastructure play, creating the potential for substantial upside if the company captures a meaningful share of the expanding stablecoin economy.
