Ink Review: Danny Boyle's Tabloid Media Origin Story Will Make You Feel Sick

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Journalism and media have undergone significant changes in the 21st century. The internet has altered everything and barriers are gone. The gatekeepers who used to be there to say what was or wasn't news don't exist, at least not in the way they used to. Director Danny Boyle has examined this "Give 'em what they want" tabloid media culture in his latest movie "Ink," which will make anyone who values real journalism sick to their stomach.

Adapting the play of the same name, the movie traces the origins of the British gossip rag The Sun, focusing on a group of misfit visionaries who had an idea for a new kind of news. Rather than simply report "the news," why not give people what they want instead?

Less a biopic a about Rupert Murdoch (played here by Guy Pearce) and his family, the real-life family the Roy clan on "Succession" is based off of, it focuses instead largely on Larry Lamb, played by "Sinners" star Jack O'Connell. Lamb was the man hand-picked by Murdoch to change The Sun from a fledgling paper into something, well, different. Driven by revenge and a sense of "I'll show 'em," Lamb, at least as presented by Boyle, is one of the most significant figures who shaped the modern era. And not in a good way, it probably goes without saying. But I'll say it anyway.

The story being told in "Ink" is an ugly one with grave consequences in the real world. Danny Boyle starts out by making this seem almost fun and exciting. Larry Lamb assembling his team has an "Ocean's Eleven" quality to it, with the kinetic energy of some of the director's best movies, most notably "Trainspotting."

Slowly but surely though, it all devolves into something more sinister. Even though news and journalism should probably be seen as a public good and an essential function of any democratic society, the need to make these publications profitable changes the game. Lamb and Rupert Murdoch lean into the "business" side of the news business, and that's where things get downright icky.

The tone of "Ink" shifts from a fun outsider tale to a dark story about taking something too far in pursuit of capitalist superiority. We see Larry Lamb lose the man he was, becoming a man that's difficult to respect. While the great newsroom movies in cinema history, such as "All the President's Men" and "Spotlight," make viewers feel the power of journalism, "Ink" forces the viewer to understand that power can and often is used in far less noble ways. Boyle is offering up his view of the men responsible for that shift.

By heading back to the past, namely the late '60s in the U.K., Boyle shines a light on the modern era. Podcasts. Blogs. Tabloids. Cable news. It all traces its roots back to this moment in history and it's hard not to leave this movie feeling some kind of way about our current media culture.

"Ink" also makes the viewer think about how accurate movies like "Spotlight" and other "based on a true story" movies are. Boyle and writer James Graham probably weren't too interested in getting everything exactly right, as this movie is about how a narrative can be shaped. Boyle and Graham shaped a narrative using real people who had real influence on the world as we know it. Accuracy isn't the point. It's about painting a picture.

"Ink" is a true showcase for Jack O'Connell. He's chewed the screen rather delightfully in movies like "28 Years Later: The Bone Temple" in recent years. But this film allows him to be more human and take the driver's seat in a grounded movie with a killer ensemble. He's the best part of a movie that also includes an excellent Claire Foy and a good-as-ever Guy Pearce.

Pearce, in particular, deserves credit for playing Rupert Murdoch with nuance. It would be easy to turn the guy who helped create Fox News into a cigar-chomping, cartoonish villain. Pearce didn't take the easy way out.

Will "Ink" rank amongst Danny Boyle's best films? Maybe not. It's tonally uneven and about as subtle as a jackhammer. But it's also got one hell of a point to make. News is kind of like vegetables: Not the most fun, but nutritious and necessary. "Ink" shines an uncomfortable light on how we, as a society, allowed news to turn from vegetables into junk food. Eat enough junk food, you'll get sick.

"Ink" hits select theatres December 11, 2026, before releasing on Netflix on January 8, 2027.

Three Bridges show spirit to earn point against Brentwood

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Bridges were desperate to get points on the board, but perhaps even more importantly, to rediscover the battling spirit that had been missing during last week's disappointing performance away at Maldon & Tiptree.

Jamie Crellin's men responded positively, showing plenty of fight and determination.

After an even opening, the hosts took the lead in the 12th minute through October's player of the month, Ade Adeyinka. David Przybylo played him through the middle and, from around 20 yards out, he unleashed an unstoppable strike into the net to give Bridges an early advantage.

The remainder of the first half was evenly matched, with Roberts producing several good saves in the Bridges goal. At the other end, the energetic Villavicencio continued to cause problems for the visitors, particularly with a dangerous cross towards Reece Hallard, whose header from six yards narrowly missed the target.

It could have been 2-0 moments into the second half as, straight from the restart, Bridges put together an excellent move down the right. Velvick released Przybylo through on goal, but under pressure from the goalkeeper, his effort narrowly drifted wide.

Brentwood began to pile on the pressure at the other end and found their reward in the 58th minute. Following a free-kick from Reid, Gibbs slotted his effort into the bottom corner to restore parity.

Just a couple of minutes later, Przybylo, who had been a constant handful for the visitors, worked his way past his marker and had another opportunity to restore Bridges' advantage, but his effort narrowly missed the far post.

With Bridges short of options on the bench and several players playing through injuries, maintaining the intensity became increasingly difficult. Brentwood Town took control during the final 20 minutes, but Roberts came to the rescue with two superb saves to deny Cerulli and Sears, while the Bridges players continued to put their bodies on the line to protect their point.

There was late drama in the 94th minute when Reece Hallard chased a ball through on the counter-attack, only for Cripps to handle it as the last man.

The Brentwood defender was shown a straight red card, leaving the visitors down to ten men. However, it came too late to have any impact on the outcome, as the match finished 1-1.

The point represents a much-needed response from Bridges, who are now into a demanding run of three consecutive away fixtures in seven days against Cray Wanderers (Tuesday 10), Welling United (Saturday 14) and Dartford (Tuesday 17).

Three Bridges: Roberts, Irving, Neathey (C), Malone, J. Hallard, Villavicencio (Chambers 87), Adeyinka, Przybylo (Richards 77), Velvick, R. Hallard, Bennett.

Player of the Match – Sam Roberts: An impressive display from Roberts in goal, producing several outstanding saves to keep Bridges in the contest. The young goalkeeper also impressed with his distribution, setting his side on the attack with some excellent kicking. A thoroughly deserved Player of the Match award.

Coinbase Joins Circle, Ripple With Final OCC Approval for Federal Trust Bank Charter

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Coinbase Joins Circle, Ripple With Final OCC Approval for Federal Trust Bank Charter

Coinbase has received final approval from the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency (OCC) to charter Coinbase National Trust Company, the crypto exchange said on Monday.

The OCC approved the application last Friday, according to Coinbase. The firm said the charter will let it expand its custody business, which holds digital assets on behalf of institutional clients, under a federal regulatory framework.

"Custody is foundational to our business," the company said, describing itself as the leading crypto custodian globally. The charter will "bring clear and consistent federal uniformity" to its custody operations, it added.

"Trusted custody is essential for bringing more of the financial system onchain," Coinbase CEO Brian Armstrong tweeted on Monday, calling the trust company "another step for building that foundation."

Trusted custody is essential for bringing more of the financial system onchain.

Coinbase National Trust Company is another step for building that foundation. https://t.co/35K2yC3xpj

— Brian Armstrong (@brian_armstrong) November 9, 2026

Coinbase has offered regulated custody since 2018, when its Coinbase Custody Trust Company won a limited purpose trust charter from the New York Department of Financial Services. The new national trust company builds on that foundation while bringing its trust activities into a consistent federal framework, Coinbase said.

The approval comes seven months after the OCC gave Coinbase conditional approval in April, when the company said it was not becoming a commercial bank and would not take retail deposits or engage in fractional reserve banking. Coinbase first applied for the charter last year.

Coinbase joins a growing list of crypto firms with national trust charters. Circle won final approval in July for a trust bank to manage the reserves behind its USDC stablecoin, and Ripple received final approval last month for a trust bank behind its RLUSD stablecoin.

Others are still awaiting final approval. Jack Dorsey's Block has applied for a charter to custody Bitcoin in September, Kraken parent Payward filed its own application in May, and a trust company linked to Trump family-backed World Liberty Financial won conditional approval in August for a charter tied to its USD1 stablecoin, Reuters reported.

The approvals have drawn opposition. Sen. Elizabeth Warren (D-MA) has argued that the OCC's crypto charter approvals, including Coinbase's, are illegal, and the Independent Community Bankers of America has sued the OCC in an effort to stop crypto firms from entering banking through what it describes as a side door.

philippe jarrigeon finds the extraordinary in nature, from twisted trees to surreal encounters

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

drawing on fashion imagery, pop culture, and surrealist staging, the french photographer creates playful scenes that challenge familiar ways of looking at the natural world.

French photographer Philippe Jarrigeon looks to the contorted beech trees of Champagne for Le Vrai du Faux, his series presented at Paris Photo 2026, where he is also the recipient of the eighth Prix Maison Ruinart.

The project follows Rocher Fatal, his second book published by RVB Books, which brings together two years of photographs made in the Forest of Fontainebleau. Moving from the woodland landscapes of Fontainebleau to the Champagne region, Jarrigeon finds inspiration in unusual natural forms, including the contorted beech trees known as the Faux de Verzy. Their twisting branches seem almost impossible to have occurred naturally, making them fitting subjects for his playful approach to photography.

Jarrigeon takes this strangeness as a starting point, bringing the trees into conversation with shells, flowers, animals, and objects. His photographs play with the idea of what nature can be, and with the curious possibility that the world might contain forms of life and ways of being that sit outside our usual understanding.

Jarrigeon’s approach to photography has developed over nearly two decades, moving between editorial commissions, commercial work, and personal projects. After graduating from ECAL (École cantonale d’art de Lausanne) in 2006, he began building a practice shaped by the visual languages of fashion, design, advertising, and popular culture.

His photographs often borrow from the polished aesthetics of fashion campaigns and the saturated imagery of 1980s and 1990s popular culture. Vivid colors and elaborate compositions give his images an immediately recognizable visual character. Yet Jarrigeon rarely uses these references straightforwardly. Humor, irony, and unexpected details introduce a sense of play and disrupt the apparent perfection of the scenes.

In Rocher Fatal, Philippe Jarrigeon spends two years photographing the Forest of Fontainebleau, approaching it as a setting for a series of curious episodes. Published by RVB Books with a text by Laura Vazquez, his second book brings together abandoned castles, dormant quarries, hikers, deer, and a fallen knight. A smiling flower and a seemingly ordinary rock also find their way into the story, alongside appearances by artists Nina Childress, Théo Mercier, Vimala Pons, Laura Vazquez, and Rebeka Warrior.

The photographs have a cinematic quality, but Jarrigeon’s sense of humor keeps the scenes from becoming too serious. The forest is at once recognizable and strangely theatrical, with its trees and inhabitants becoming part of the same narrative. In the years following the photographs, fires damaged parts of the Fontainebleau forest, affecting its trees, habitats, and well-used paths. The book also recalls a landscape that has since undergone significant damage.

In Le Vrai du Faux, Jarrigeon continues this exploration in the Champagne region, where geological history and living forms become material for an imaginative photographic project. The Faux de Verzy, with their unusually twisted trunks and branches, are among its central subjects. Their shapes give the trees a supernatural presence, and Jarrigeon photographs them as though they were characters.

Other images introduce shells, remnants of a sea that once covered the region, alongside bouquets, luminous forms, and ambiguous figures. In The Harvest, Poppy, and The Surprise, familiar natural elements take on an unfamiliar character through composition and staging. The presence of animals and human figures complicates the distinction between observer and subject, while objects appear to belong as naturally to the landscape as the plants and creatures around them.

Jarrigeon describes the project as an invitation to look beyond nature as scenery. ‘With Le Vrai du Faux, nature is never treated as a mere backdrop; it becomes a source of inspiration and a space for imagination. My experience of Champagne has shaped a vision of landscapes inhabited by presences, spirits, remnants of the past, and fictions,’ the photographer notes.

Jarrigeon avoids the usual solemn approach to nature, creating images in which trees, flowers, shells, animals, and man-made objects appear side by side. The combinations are often unexpected, giving familiar elements a different character.

The photographs also move away from a human-centered view of the natural world. People are only one presence among many, sharing the scenes with plants, animals, and objects. Jarrigeon leaves the relationships between them open, allowing the images to move between the recognizable and the absurd.

Le Vrai du Faux is presented by WOMANRAY in the Émergence section of Paris Photo, taking place at the Grand Palais from November 12 to 15, 2026. The project also marks Jarrigeon’s selection for the Prix Maison Ruinart. Meanwhile, his solo exhibition Rocher Fatal is on view at WOMANRAY in Paris through December 24, bringing together work from his recent exploration of the Forest of Fontainebleau.

upcoming presentation: Le Vrai du Faux – Paris Photo 2026, Émergence section venue: Grand Palais, Paris, France dates: November 12–15, 2026

Geyoro inspires London City Lionesses to 4-1 WSL win over Birmingham City

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

To help deliver timely coverage of women's football, we may use AI tools to support content creation and editing. While we strive for accuracy and editorial quality, readers should independently verify important information.

London City Lionesses ended a four-match winless run against Birmingham City on Sunday 8 November, winning 4-1 in the Women’s Super League. Eder Maestre’s side led 2-0 at half-time in Dartford and finished with 20 shots to Birmingham’s 4. Their last win over Birmingham came on 12 February 2023.

Poppy Pattinson opened the scoring after 10 minutes. Six minutes later Delphine Cascarino doubled the lead from a Grace Geyoro assist. Cascarino was booked for a foul in the 27th minute, and María Pérez followed her into the referee’s notebook in the 37th.

Amy Merricks’s side halved the deficit five minutes after the restart. Océane Hurtré scored in the 50th minute, set up by Lisa Naalsund. It was Birmingham’s only shot on target all afternoon, and Hurtré’s display earned her a rating of 8.2.

Neve Grace Herron was cautioned for dissent in the 72nd minute. Two minutes later Geyoro scored herself, restoring the two-goal margin. With a goal and an assist, she finished rated 8.82, the highest on the pitch.

Daniela Arques was booked in the 81st minute, the hosts’ third yellow card. The last word went to Kadidiatou Diani, who came off the bench for 45 minutes and scored in the second minute of stoppage time from a Freya Godfrey assist. That was Diani’s fourth league goal of the season, eighth in the WSL scoring chart.

The home side dominated the numbers. They had 67% of the ball, completed 604 of 683 passes and put 8 efforts on target, forcing 4 saves from Baggaley. Earps did not have a save to make. Expected goals finished 1.52 to 0.19.

The win leaves London City Lionesses third with 19 points from 9 games, 4 behind Chelsea and 6 behind leaders Manchester City. It was their fourth win in six matches in all competitions, coming straight after a 2-1 defeat by Manchester City.

Birmingham are 13th on 6 points, 6 clear of Charlton Athletic. This was a second straight 4-1 defeat after the loss to Tottenham Hotspur. Liverpool, in 12th, are 2 points ahead.

Birmingham have a quick chance to respond. They host Sunderland in the WSL Cup on Wednesday 11 November, while London City Lionesses travel to Arsenal in the league on Saturday 14 November.

Speedrunners Didn't Take Long To Find New Ways To Break Ocarina Of Time

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Ocarina of Time has only been out on Switch 2 for a matter of days, but that has proven more than enough time for the speedrunning community to find a bunch of new exploits in it (thanks for the heads-up, IGN).

Within the first few hours, those who know the N64 game inside and out were hard at work finding ways to cut corners or otherwise break the game to wrap things up as quickly as possible.

Zelda speedrunner gymnast89 managed to hit credits in as little as four and a half hours on the day after the game's launch (we were still marvelling at Kokiri Forest's water effects at that point), and the skip techniques have only multiplied since then.

The biggie at the time of writing is a new glitch that sends Link soaring into the sky, bypassing a bunch of puzzles and looking particularly badass as he does so. Speedrunner ZFG was seemingly the first to discover the move, which relies on some careful positioning around the game's Magic Bean flowers. A series of hops and skips with the camera angle just right, and the game's physics system goes into panic mode, giving Link a real impressive vertical jump.

It doesn't seem to be tied to Shadow Temple skips, either. Other players have found that you can replicate a similar move from the comfort of an empty chest, flitting between Ocarina tracks or using specific items to once again send Link on the up and up.

But what about those sections of the speedrun where Link needs to be on Terra Firma? The remake boasts a brand new dash button for quicker on-foot travel, but there must be an even quicker way, right?

Well, it looks like it! u/Pomps8a took to Reddit with the following strat, which lets Link roll quicker by interrupting the animation with an attack at the end. It's perhaps not the most elegant solution, and there doesn't seem to be any one-to-one matchup at the time of writing to see how much quicker it is, but anything that helps shave off valuable milliseconds is always handy to those in the know.

There's another glitch doing the rounds that lets speedrunners hop over previously inaccessible ledges by item swapping in mid-air. So, if the above big jump is too much, then at least they've got the option of a smaller big jump.

And for those wondering, no, all of this manipulation doesn't work wonders for Link's physical well-being. Skipping big chunks of the game means that a bunch of the remake's new cutscenes are thrown off whack — with Child Link appearing in Adult Link clips, and vice versa — and the Hero of Time looks all the worse for it.

Just look at the poor boy in the following post-sequence break screens from ZFG's recent speedrunning stream:

Honestly, we've been laughing at the above 'Lonk' and 'Lunk' for longer than we care to admit. Look at his little face!

We knew it wouldn't take long for the speedrunning community to find ways to break the remake, but we'll admit that this many glitches so early is rather impressive. We're sure that even more will be unearthed in the coming days/months/years.

Even without all of the glitches, Ocarina of Time is still a damn good time on Switch 2. We awarded the game a 9/10 in our review, calling it "unquestionably the best way to play one of the most seminal games of all time".

West Ham face Taty Castellanos, River Plate transfer exit threat

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

As West Ham continue to fall short of rekindling their fortunes despite Scott Parker's recent appointment, the possibility of Taty Castellanos departing the London Stadium in the coming months has now emerged in a development that will spark mixed reactions from Hammers supporters.

West Ham's return to the Championship following 14 successive seasons of solid Premier League standing is not going to plan right now, with the East Londoners appearing increasingly unlikely to seal a previously-expected top-flight return at the very first time of asking as champions.

A mixed run of form featuring consecutive home defeats at home to a pair of promotion rivals in Southampton and West Bromwich Albion ultimately ended Nuno Espirito Santo's unpopular tenure in charge, and the Portuguese boss has been swiftly succeeded by former Hammers midfielder Parker.

The ex-Bournemouth and Fulham boss, having already achieved three promotions from the second-tier with as many clubs after most recently guiding Burnley back to the promised land in 2025 before his dismissal towards the end of the previous campaign, has encountered a challenging return to the club thus far, though, taking just one point from his opening two matches.

Last Wednesday's thrilling 3-3 comeback draw at Cardiff City after overturning a three-goal defecit at half-time was followed up by a disappointing 2-1 defeat away to Blackburn Rovers over the weekend, and with the Hammers yet to enjoy the anticipated upturn under Parker, they now face the threat of losing one of their big-name performers once the January window rolls around.

Castellanos has endured a particularly difficult and underwhelming introduction to the Championship, which the Argentine forward was expected to light up after his mid-season arrival back in January preceded encouraging goalscoring form in spite of his side's ultimate failure to dodge the drop.

Six goals from 18 Premier League appearances means that the current return of just one strike across 14 second-tier fixtures - nine of which have been starts - is not what fans were anticipating, and they had presented vocal criticism towards the divisive 28-year-old for a number of weeks before Parker hauled the attacker off at the interval against the Bluebirds and then dropped his name out of the starting line-up at Ewood Park.

It has now emerged that Castellanos could leave East London as early as January, with a fresh report from Argentine-based outlet El Intransigente claiming strong long-term interest from River Plate.

As per the update, River Plate have held interest in the two-cap Argentine international's services in recent windows and he represents a "top target" in the eyes of manager Leonardo Ponzio.

Blackburn Rovers captain Todd Cantwell has been among those reacting to the club's dig towards Scott Parker's West Ham after Saturday's 2-1 victory

It's said that the Buenos Aries-based giants feel strongly about Castellano's profile and international experience, with the striker having risen to prominence outside of his native Argentina in Uruguay with Montevideo City Torque before enjoying productive spells at New York City, Girona and Lazio ahead of joining West Ham earlier this year for a reported £26 million figure on a four-and-a-half year contract.

West Ham supporters have grown increasingly critical of Castellanos and his long-term future under Parker remains unclear at this moment in time, although a fresh hurdle could prevent River Plate from sealing the deal.

River Plate have been presented with a significant obstacle to overcome if they intend on returning Castellanos to his homeland, as it's a move which wouldn't come cheaply for the 38-time Primera Division champions.

Indeed, the report has added that the salary Castellanos earns at the London Stadium is the main challenge for River Plate right now, leaving the club to weigh up alternative options such as acquiring the sought-after frontman on loan or even purchasing a percentage of his rights.

Both of those options, of course, would require the prior approval of West Ham, whose own stance on potentially parting ways with the ex-Lazio striker just a year after his arrival with a club likely unable to recoup the figure of purchase is also not yet known.

It's noted that Castellanos' lengthy contract with the Championship promotion hopefuls puts Parker's side in a comfortable negotiating decision, and hold all the cards as River Plate will not be able to wrap up any deal without their support.

Interestingly, however, the update also makes reference to previous interest elsewhere, reminding that Castellanos had reportedly wished to leave West Ham in the summer amid loan interest from Seville-based rivals Real Betis and Sevilla that never materialised.

The London Stadium faithful remain very much divided on Castellanos at the moment, with some making their minds up on the out-of-form forward and others willing to persist with further patience.

Prior claims of a January exit if the price is met at the end of the previous month saw some West Ham fans refuse to hold back on their feelings about Castellanos' future, and this one fan believes that his hapless form in the Championship would make for no great loss if he was to depart.

Another would be happy to see the Hammers striker leave at any cost, even if the club themselves surely won't take that stance.

Castellanos has left some frustrated for reasons beyond his inability to find the back of the net on a regular basis, too, and having formerly scored at a relatively-prolific rate leading the line for a side relegated from the Premier League, his work-rate and application have been called into question.

His display in the aforementioned draw at Cardiff drew particular criticism, and West Ham were a much more fluid, functional and threatening outfit once the Argentine was withdrawn for Joel Piroe at the break.

Now, with the Suriname international scoring in successive encounters, it's unclear how Castellanos will force his way back into starting contention ahead of a positional rival with a historically-goal-laden hit-rate at this level.

West Ham fans, how do you feel about Taty Castellanos' future? Would you hold onto the striker, or is it time to cash in? What should his valuation be? Let us know your thoughts in the comments below!

But there are other fans who continue to stand by Castellanos, and considering how he fared at the highest level last time out, it's no great surprise that certain supporters still believe he can come good in the second-tier.

Either way, there are plenty of West Ham fans who, despite the criticism of the majority, do feel that the divisive striker would thrive in a system that emphasises chance creation under a manager willing to trust in his services and get the best out of an undeniably-talented striker with pedigree in more prestigious and higher-quality divisions.

This punter, meanwhile, reckons that cashing in on Castellanos would be a "stupid decision", but only time will tell to see what action West Ham decide to take and whether a deal can successfully be thrashed out with River Plate.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

Thoughts of a lifelong fan: Where to start?

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Where to start? Yesterday was not good enough. It wasn’t good enough from the players, the coach and yes, from some of our supporters too. When we arrived at Wimborne Stadium, after the half mile walk from the car park, spirits were high with some fans predicting an easy victory, whereas I would have taken a win on penalties if it had been on offer there and then. We all know we didn’t get that; we couldn’t blame the referee as none of the ref’s decisions would have affected the result, and we have to accept that on the day Wimborne wanted it more than we appeared to.

I hope you have noticed the use of “we” in my writing, because that’s what following and supporting any team in any sport should be about. I was at Old Trafford when we went a goal down and the level of support we gave increased, I was also at Wrexham when we lost four-one in the promotion season and were complimented by the Welsh fans on how we stuck with our team throughout the whole game.

Did you know with an ad-lite subscription to SussexWorld, you get 70% fewer ads while viewing the news that matters to you.

The difference yesterday? Apart from some players giving their all there appeared to be no plan of action to get the right result. I can’t accept it was all down to the players though, as the coach would seem to suggest. I believe that we have enough quality players in our bottom of the table side, not to be where we currently sit at the nadir of the 92 and out of all the cups that really should matter to us. I believe that supporting a team must be shown more when things aren’t going right, and if that means cheering your team on even when they are losing to a poor side, who would normally be beaten easily, then so be it.

Nobody covered themselves in glory yesterday, the coach, players and us the fans.  Some of the “us” were too quiet whilst some chose to be negatively noisy, demolishing, rather than supporting the players on the pitch.

You can tell I’m not a happy devil, but will I be there at the next game to cheer the players on? Yes, I will, and I’ll keep on supporting them no matter what.

PS Why wasn’t Ade Adeyemo brought on Yesterday Kevin, when I believe he could have made the difference.

Heysel ’85 is the definitive, eye-opening drama that finally looks past Liverpool terrace narrative

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Play the viral 442GOALS game and build an XI of Premier League legends!

Sign up to our FREE live football newsletter, tracking all of the biggest games available to watch on the device of your choice. Never miss a kick-off!

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

FourFourTwo attended the 2026 London Film Festival for the screening of 'Heysel 85', which will be released in cinemas this November

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

For the past 40 years, the tragedy of the 1985 European Cup final has often been reduced in mainstream British discourse to a single, monolithic headline: football hooliganism.

In Heysel ’85, director Teodora Mihai bypasses the familiar cliches of terrace culture to produce something which seeks to change the conversation surrounding the disaster. Coming from a documentary background, Mihai approaches the disaster not as a reflection of senseless violence, but as a study into institutional breakdown and the callous, self-preserving instincts of political power.

The film anchors its narrative through two parallel viewpoints. The principal perspective belongs to Marie Dumont (Violet Braeckman), the fictional press attaché and daughter to the Mayor of Brussels.

Braeckman, a polyglot who learned Italian specifically for the production, delivers a performance of quiet, escalating dread as she watches the veneer of civic competence crumble from within.

Operating on the other side of the crisis is Luca Rossi (Matteo Simoni), an Italian broadcast journalist reporting live from the ground while desperately trying to locate his father, uncle, and brother, all attending the match as Juventus supporters.

Early on, Mihai builds tension through procedural unease. Disorienting, fluid tracking shots wind through subterranean corridors, establishing a sense of foreboding long before the tragic events unfold. Shot in an empty hospital in Antwerp, the oppressive concrete hallways reflect the popular view that the Heysel Stadium, built in 1930, was run-down and unfit for a fixture of such magnitude by the mid-1980s, something which is referenced in the film.

The film avoids the trap of laying blame at the feet of ticket touts, generic mobs, or rowdy Liverpool supporters. It is deeply sympathetic to the ordinary fans caught up in the events of May 29, 1985, casting a forensic eye upward at the organisers.

The best features, fun and footballing quizzes, straight to your inbox every week.

UEFA executives, the local municipal government, and a fractured security apparatus are all painted in an unflattering light. The territorial friction between the municipal police and the Belgian Gendarmerie is laid bare; their reluctance and inability to share intelligence or coordinate chain-of-command turns an already combustible situation into an avoidable tragedy.

Nowhere is this administrative failure more grotesque than in the depiction of the Mayor of Brussels (played by Josse De Pauw). Portrayed as a sozzled, cowardly executive, his characterisation is rooted in historical reality. According to Braeckman, De Pauw knew the real-life mayor Hervé Brouhon, a figure regarding whom it was openly joked in local political circles that any serious decisions had to be made before 11am.

The film captures the hollow facade of authority dissipating in direct proportion to his alcohol intake. In one particularly telling sequence early on, Marie is dispatched to fetch the key to the stadium’s liquor cabinet so the mayor and his dignitaries can enjoy a proper drink. It is a bitterly ironic snapshot of municipal priorities just hours before 39 people would lose their lives.

When catastrophe strikes, the narrative strips away any remaining shred of civil propriety. Mihai’s documentary sensibilities ensure the integration of genuine, unaltered archive footage feels seamless rather than exploitative, anchoring the fictional protagonists in historical reality. While the Italian delegation emerges with a degree of grace and anguish, UEFA and local authorities retreat into corporate damage control, consumed entirely by liability, protocol, and the insistence that the match must still be played.

By refusing to take the easy narrative route of sensationalising terrace thuggery, Heysel ’85 delivers an important, unsettling reassessment of the catastrophe. It is an indictment of systemic cowardice that proves human lives were not lost merely to crowd surges, but to the arrogance of an establishment that abdicated responsibility long before kick-off - and, tellingly, never resigned in its wake.

Following its UK premiere at the 70th BFI London Film Festival, Heysel ’85 will be released in UK cinemas on 20 November 2026, distributed by Icon Film Distribution.

Joe joined FourFourTwo as senior digital writer in July 2025 after five years covering Leeds United in the Championship and Premier League. Joe's 'Mastermind' specialist subject is 2000s-era Newcastle United having had a season ticket at St. James' Park for 10 years before relocating to Leeds and later London. Joe takes a keen interest in youth football, covering PL2, U21 Euros, as well as U20 and U17 World Cups in the past, in addition to hosting the industry-leading football recruitment-focused SCOUTED podcast. He is also one of the lucky few to have 'hit top bins' as a contestant on Soccer AM. It wasn't a shin-roller.

We Keep Going Back To Germany. This Family Trip Reminded Me Why.

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

We landed in Zürich on SAS and drove to my in-law’s home in Southwest Germany, about an hour away.

There are border checks now as you enter Germany from France and Switzerland, though usually (I’m just saying…) when they see it’s white people they just waive you through.

And I’ll leave off the politics here (because I’d rather focus on getting our own house in order in the USA), but I think this picture says it all about Germany these days:

We tend to stop at DM (a drug store chain) at the start of each trip because it’s on the way home and we always come with a list since so many products here are cheaper and better than their American counterparts, even with the stronger Euro. One thing I like about DM (well, liked…) was that they had a free water dispenser with cups.

In Germany water is an annoyance when going out…most restaurants and coffee shops frown on tap water (unlike in France and the Netherlands), so you’re paying everywhere €5-8EUR for bottles you can buy for 50-80 Euro cents in the grocery store. It’s nice to “hydrate” at DM, but for TWO YEARS the water machine has been broken.

How in the heck can you not fix this for two years (unless, as I suppose, the point is that you don’t want to offer free water any longer)?!

It was a bit warm for the time of the year, but not unpleasant. How nice it was to be back and over the next several days we enjoyed a lot of quality time together, with good döner, coffee, and ice cream.

There was a fairly intense lighting storm that was just beautiful to sit out on the patio and watch:

One morning I decided to ride by myself to Basel for coffee…there was a new place I wanted to try called Mantila Coffee…it was SO GOOD (my new favorite coffee spot in Basel) and I had such a nice chat with the owner. I’m going to update my Basel Coffee post with a couple of places I tried on this trip.

Augustine was livid that biked to Basel without him, so we returned the next day (sadly Mantila is closed on Saturday), but we found another great place called Tinka. Augustine wanted his own coffee…I had a cortado, he had cappuccino, and then we split a delicious sparkling blood orange drink.

My father-in-law likes a restaurant called Gasthaus Krone in the hamlet of Lehnacker, located really right in the middle of the Schwarzwald (Black Forest). The food is delicious indeed.

One cool part of this trip is that my daughter finally got the hang of riding her bike herself…we just don’t feel it’s safe to ride bikes at home (due to fast drivers…see here for a laugh) but it was fun to see her so excited.

To celebrate, we had (more) ice cream together.

Finally, to be clear, the trip did not happen this weekend. I delay these reports for my own privacy and the protection of my family…as I’ve mentioned before, sadly there are monsters out there (I’ll just leave it at that…). But what a lovely and unexpected getaway this was and I am so grateful for all the time spent in Germany.

Time in the city and time in the country. Travel and family. Time spent in community (faith based or otherwise). Coffee. All are non-optional for a well lived life. Hope you enjoyed the trip.

U15 : de la joie contre les Caladois

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Les U15 de Benjamin Petit pouvaient être heureux à la fin de leur match contre les Caladois du FC Villefranche Beaujolais. A l'issue d'une rencontre très disputée, comme souvent dans cet exigeant championnat, ils n'ont rien lâché face à un adversaire de valeur et ont su trouver les ressources pour l'emporter. Cela récompense leur volonté d'aller de l'avant, leur ténacité et leur concentration dans l'aspect défensif. Sans quelques joueurs retenus avec les U16 de Kevin De Jesus (Ilan Clapeyron, Riad Moutae, Kaïs Boukerma et Hugo Durin) tous ont su se mettre au diapason, et cette victoire est celle d'un collectif très solidaire.

L'entame du match a été stéphanoise, les Verts s'installant dans le camp caladois. Dès la 9ème minute, sur un centre venu de la gauche, Nolan Lebreton remettait pour Charly Davignon qui était devancé de peu par un défenseur. Deux minutes plus tard, un coup-franc de ce même Nolan Lebreton était capté par le gardien. Les Verts monopolisaient le ballon mais le dernier geste, souvent dans les petits espaces, manquait de justesse pour mettre de conclure. Peu à peu, Villefranche déserrait l'étreinte stéphanoise et se montrait dangereux sur coup de pied arrêté, mais Manoa Frison faisait bonne garde, notamment, à la 26ème, où il réalisait une double parade avant que Victor Hamouma ne s'interpose pour stopper la tentative adverse. Quelques minutes plus tard, Charly Davignon, qui repiquait au centre, était victime d'une faute laquelle donnait lieu à un coup-franc. Nolhan Fernandes le frappait et mettait le gardien à contribution. Peu après sur un long centre de Nolhan Fernandes, Hugo Bastide obtenait un corner. Ce corner, joué à deux, permettait à Nolan Lebreton d'adresser un long centre au deuxième poteau que le portier caladois dégageait. Les contres de Villefranche butaient sur une solide défense stéphanoise, concentrée et rigoureuse qui gérait bien les espaces et s'attachait à ressortir proprement les ballons. Le score était donc nul et vierge au repos.

La deuxième mi-temps repartait sur les mêmes bases, avec une maitrîse verte retrouvée et des situations intéressantes, sans parvenir toutefois à faire la différence.  Mais les Verts, suite à un carton blanc à la 54ème minute, se retrouvaient réduits à 10 pendant une dizaine de minutes, et ce fait de jeu freinait un peu leur dynamique. Ils allaient trouver la faille suite à un cafouillage dans la surface, avec une main adverse et un accrochage sur Mathis La Rosa. Toujours est-il que l'arbitre sanctionnait cette action d'un penalty que Charly Davignon transformait, donnant l'avantage aux Verts (68ème). La fin du match allait être plutôt hâchée avec plusieurs coups-de-pied arrêtés que Villefranche jouait directement. Les Verts se créaient cependant quelques opportunités mais un manque de justesse ne leur permettait pas de les concrétiser. Le match se terminait donc sur cette victoire stéphanoise somme toute logique, tant elle avait été bien construite.

La composition : Frison - Bergougnoux (Uneau, 50ème), L. La Rosa, Hamouma, Bastide - Wojcik (Davignon, 50ème), M.La Rosa, Lebreton - Fernandes (Madjidi, 50ème), Maganga Verrier, Davignon (Linganzi, 41ème).

La réaction de Benjamin Petit : "Le match a été serré, très disputé face à une bonne équipe de Villefranche, comme souvent dans ce championnat. Il n'y a pas de match facile et c'est un bon apprentissage pour les jeunes. Nous avons fait vingt premières minutes plutôt intéressantes, avec de la maîtrise, une volonté de défendre en avançant, un point que travaillons. Nous avons baissé en fin de première mi-temps, avec plus de difficulté à maintenir notre pression, ce qui nous a menés à défendre plus bas. En conclusion, je dirais que globalement, nous avons eu la maîtrise, mais mon regret est le manque de justesse, de percussion et d'efficacité pour créer du danger sur le but adverse et se mettre à l'abri. Je retiens notre solidité défensive, notre rigueur dans les prises en charge, notre détermination à ne rien encaisser, notamment sur les coups-de-pied arrêtés, un domaine dans lequel nous avons progressé. Il faudra néanmoins éviter certaines fautes, évitables, qui offrent des situations dangereuses à l'adversaire. C'était un match intéressant dans l'apprentissage, la gestion des temps forts et des temps faibles. Il faut toutefois  essayer d'être plus efficace dans nos temps forts tout en restant solides dans les temps faibles."

Scotland international Ryan Porteous celebrated his recall to the national team by grabbing a rare assist for LAFC, but was left to rue an early mistake as his side fell to a 2-1 away defeat against Seattle Sounders on Sunday night.

Porteous, 27, was named in the Scotland squad by head coach Sebastien Pocognoli as an injury replacement for Stephen Welsh at the weekend, with the Swansea City defender ruled out after picking up an injury in the 3-1 EFL Championship defeat to Portsmouth last Tuesday.

It means Porteous could make his first Scotland appearance since he was introduced as a late substitute in the 1-0 Nations League play-off win in Greece back in March 2025, as the national team prepares for a double-header against North Macedonia and Switzerland later this week.

The former Hibs centre-back added to his successful week by picking up his first assist of the season for Los Angeles FC as they fell to a narrow defeat at Seattle in their last regular season game. However, the clash began in disastrous fashion for the visitors, with Slovakian international Albert Rusnak putting Seattle into the lead after just 52 seconds at Lumen Field.

With Los Angeles in possession of the ball from kick-off, Hugo Lloris’ attempt to pass the ball back to Porteous caused confusion, with the Scottish defender and Mathieu Choiniere appearing to get in each other’s way. The latter lost the ball under pressure, allowing Cristian Roldan to pinch the ball and cross for Rusnak to tap into an empty net to put the home side into an early lead.

The home side then added a second just after the half-hour mark through winger Sebastian Gomez, with the 20-year-old’s shot from just inside the area deflecting into the top corner beyond Lloris to put the hosts into a commanding first-half lead as they looked to get a win that would seal their place in the end-of-season MLS playoffs, which begins later this month.

LAFC did their best to launch a comeback, with the visitors having 13 attempts on goal in the second 45 to Seattle’s zero. However, they couldn’t to find a breakthrough until the 82nd minute, with Porteous rising highest to Leo Walta’s free-kick, and heading across goal for centre-back partner Eddie Segura to bullet home and reduce the deficit.

In the end, though, the second-half onslaught was not enough to rescue a point in the Emerald City, as LAFC ended the regular Western Conference season in sixth place on 47 points, with 12 wins, 11 draws and 11 defeats, setting up a round of 16 clash with St. Louis City in the MLS playoffs on November 23.

“Conceding a goal inside the first minute completely changes the game plan,” lamented LAFC defender Segura. “We can't keep putting ourselves in a position where we are chasing matches from the opening whistle. It is a bad habit we have to fix immediately before the playoffs start.”

Declan Rice reveals he's stepped back from social media because it can 'ruin your whole day' after sick trolling of him and his girlfriend - as he poses in new photoshoot

By LUKE AUGUSTUS, ASSISTANT SPORTS EDITOR

Declan Rice has opened up about reducing his use of social media - citing the mental toll it can have.

The Arsenal and England midfielder has been subjected to online abuse in the past - with his long-term partner Lauren Fryer having also been targeted.

Online abuse about her appearance had led to Fryer wiping all posts from her Instagram page back in 2024.

While Rice still has an Instagram account, he quit X three years ago as a result of the negativity that app can bring.

'I've not been back on the app since,' he told GQ Magazine - after being named among their Men of the Year. 'You could be having a great morning. You read one thing, and it just ruins your whole day.

'I feel like that's one of my strongest points... the ability to block out noise and not deal with that kind of stuff.'

Declan Rice has revealed he quit X three years ago due to the abuse he would receive online

Rice and long-term partner Lauren Fryer have been targeted in the past (pair pictured with their son Jude in May)

The midfielder says one post could 'ruin' his whole day - even if he was a great day beforehand

Rice, who has a four-year-old son called Jude with Fryer, is worried about the impact of social media on the younger generations.

'Screen time, for me, isn't a big thing,' he says of his own personal phone usage. 'I think social media for kids is 100 per cent a problem. I don't feel like there's as much personality now as there was a few years ago.'

As detailed by Daily Mail Sport last month, the 27-year-old is closing in on a new long-term deal at Arsenal believed to be worth around £350,000 per week.

The contract will put him on a level footing with Bukayo Saka at the top of the club's salary list.

And speaking about money in football as a whole, Rice told GQ Magazine that the finances within the sport are 'ridiculous' and despite his wealth he still wants to set a good example to his son Jude.

'The money that's in football as a whole is just ridiculous,' he continued. 'It's important to understand what things cost, even if you don't have to worry about paying for them.

'You still want to teach your kid values, not wanting them to be spoiled. You want them to work and to understand the value of life and money.

'It makes you more comfortable to be able to do things. But I don't wake up and think, "Oh, I've got loads of money", it's not the end goal.'

What Your Peace Lily Needs in November – 5 Winter Wins for Glossy New Growth and Sparkling Blooms

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Beautiful plants and ideas to brighten your garden.

What Your Christmas Cactus Needs in November – Ace These 4 Tasks for Fabulous Holiday Flowers

What Your Hydrangeas Need in November – 5 Ways to Get Plants Winter-Ready and Kickstart Your Best Blooms Ever

These 7 Plants Need Pruning Before Winter – November is Your Last Chance to Make the Cut

Sign up for the Gardening Know How newsletter today and receive a free copy of our e-book "How to Grow Delicious Tomatoes".

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Peace lilies may be entering a slower growth phase, but a few quick checks can make or break the healthy and vitality of your favorite houseplants over winter

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Your peace lily (Spathiphyllum) may well have spent the early part of fall thriving in its regular corner with little more than a weekly drink. But this can all change sharply when November arrives. Suddenly, the lower leaves droop, that trademark glossy sheen can look dull, and the leaf tips might turn dry and brown, even if you haven't changed your routine. The real shift is taking place in the house around the plant, and that’s easy to miss when the plant hasn’t moved an inch.

Peace lilies are naturally resilient, but they are sensitive to environmental shifts. The days are shorter, the sunlight angles have dropped, and central heating systems are kicking into gear. Good peace lily care through winter is largely about keeping up with those changes. The good news is that the fixes are small. It’s just about recognizing that your plant’s needs change as winter starts to set in.

By tweaking your habits and pausing unnecessary chores that may have been essential just a month earlier, you can protect root health and ensure your peace lily remains a centerpiece of lush green calm throughout these next few chilly months. Not only will these adjustments protect your favorite houseplant in winter, they will help to set the stage for more striking white spathes come spring. So here’s how to keep your peace lily healthy once the heat comes on…

Indoor winter living often creates conditions that clash with tropical houseplant health. We seal our doors, turn up the furnace, and seek out cozy, dry warmth. For a peace lily, that same forced-air heating strips ambient room humidity down to single digits while leaving drafty windowpanes ice-cold. Coexisting happily indoors through winter means balancing these conditions, providing the warmth your peace lily needs while protecting it against parched air, dark corners, and soggy soil.

Diagnosing leaf symptoms early in November prevents minor seasonal stress from escalating into root damage. If your entire plant suddenly collapses into a limp heap, check the soil before reaching for the watering can. If the potting mix is bone dry, a thorough drink will perk the foliage up within hours. If the mix feels soggy and heavy while the leaves hang limp, waterlogged roots are suffocating: stop watering now!

Brown, crispy leaf tips usually stem from low humidity, uneven moisture, or mineral salt accumulation from tap water. Occasional yellowing on a leaf lower down is normal ageing. However, multiple yellow leaves can indicate insufficient light or waterlogged compost or insufficient light. A long-probe soil moisture meter can help you determine deeper root moisture. I’m a big fan of the XLUX Long Probe Soil Moisture Meter, available from Amazon, which delivers instant readings, so you can address overwatering and soggy potting mix before fungal rot sets in.

As for flowers, don't expect too many right now. Winter usually isn't when peace lilies bloom. A plant that rarely flowers at all, though, probably needs a brighter spot, unless it's still young (under a year old, some won't bloom yet). Knowing some of the warning signs, hopefully the following tasks will help you avoid calamity this winter…

Sign up for the Gardening Know How newsletter today and receive a free copy of our e-book "How to Grow Delicious Tomatoes".

The quickest way to harm a peace lily in November is sticking to a rigid summer watering schedule. As natural day length shortens and light intensity wanes, your plant enters a semi-dormant rest period. Transpiration slows, cell division halts, and roots need less moisture from the soil. A potting blend that dried out in 5 days during July can easily stay damp for 3 weeks in November. Keeping the soil constantly saturated starves roots of oxygen, eventually leading to Pythium root rot.

This step is easy: insert a finger a couple of inches (5cm) into the potting mix. Water only when it feels dry and crumbly to the touch. When you do water your peace lily, soak the root ball thoroughly with room-temperature water until it drains freely from the base, then empty any runoff collected in the saucer. Never leave a peace lily sitting in stagnant, chilled water. If your plant droops, check soil dampness first.

A nifty way of reducing the risks associated with poor drainage is to give your plants some elevation in the drainage tray. Kakuna Plant Pot Feet, available from Amazon, can be inserted between the pot and tray to minimize chills and waterlogging.

Peace lilies have an enduring reputation as indestructible low-light houseplants, but there is a difference between surviving and thriving. While they tolerate the subdued light of a shaded summer room, the dramatic drop in November’s daylight hours and sun intensity can throw low-light corners into near-darkness. Without help, your peace lily cannot produce the energy needed to support its broad leaf surface, leading to faded foliage, drooping stems, and a halt in flower spathe production.

Take time now to reassess where your peace lily sits and check how much light reaches the plant around noon. If needed, move the plant closer to an east or north-facing window, where it can absorb hours of bright, indirect winter daylight. Keep the leaves a few inches (8-10cm) back from direct contact with windowpanes, as freezing outdoor temperatures radiate through cold glass and will chill leaf tissue.

If placing your plant near a south or west-facing window where bright afternoon sun beams through, hang a sheer curtain to diffuse the rays; direct, unfiltered sunlight can still scorch foliage even in winter. For darker rooms lacking bright exposures, supplemental artificial light provides an easy fix. The GE BR30 Grow LED Light Bulb from Walmart fits a standard socket and lamp fixture, emitting balanced, warm-toned full-spectrum light that sustains photosynthesis through dark winter months.

Indoor central heating is a double hazard for peace lilies: it strips moisture from the air, while creating extreme temperature pockets. Peace lilies thrive in relative humidity levels between 50% and 60%. When heating systems get switched on, indoor humidity frequently plunges below 20%. This dry air saps moisture from leaf margins, causing crispy brown edges and papery tips. Position your plant away from forced-air vents, radiators, space heaters, and wood stoves. The blast of hot air will quickly scorch foliage. At the same time, keep plants shielded from exterior door drafts and chilly single-pane windows, maintaining a range of 65-80°F (18-27°C).

Skip leaf misting, which provides only a brief humidity spike and can encourage fungal leaf spotting. Instead, place your pot on a wide tray filled with pebbles and water (keeping the pot base resting above the water line). Alternatively, group multiple houseplants together, or run a small ultrasonic room humidifier nearby. To help you track things, buy a digital sensor like the TempPro TP50 Digital Room Temperature and Humidity Monitor, available from The Home Depot. This compact digital sensor monitors relative humidity and ambient temperature fluctuations, helping you pinpoint cold drafts and heating hotspots.

During the dark days of late fall and winter, feeding a peace lily does far more harm than good. Because diminished daylight slows metabolic activity, the plant is not producing new tissue or developing roots. Adding liquid houseplant fertilizer during this semi-dormant time means mineral salts can build up in the potting soil. Over several weeks, this salt buildup can burn roots and brown leaf tips. Now is the time to withhold all supplemental feeding until active spring growth returns in late March.

Similarly, postpone repotting until the warmer, brighter days of spring. It doesn’t matter if you see a few roots poking out of the drainage holes: peace lilies tolerate being slightly pot-bound and recover faster in spring. Moving a sluggish plant into a larger pot now can shock them. The only exception is emergency repotting. If a plant has collapsed from severe root rot, gently tip it from its pot and rinse away old soil. Trim off brown mushy roots with sterile shears, and repot into fresh, well-aerated potting mix. A great lightweight blend for emergency winter repotting is Espoma Organic Houseplant Potting Soil Mix, available at The Home Depot.

In just a few weeks, broad peace lily leaves collect a fine film of household dust, pet dander, and airborne grease. Dusty foliage can be severely limiting in winter. Dust acts as a barrier, scattering what little winter light reaches the leaves and reducing the plant's already limited photosynthetic capacity. Cleaning the foliage is a quick, high-reward task that keeps leaves breathing and photosynthesizing efficiently. Support each leaf from beneath with one hand, and gently wipe the upper and lower surfaces with a soft cloth moistened with room-temperature water.

As you clean, inspect the leaf undersides and stem junctions for pests. Dry winter heating can encourage rapid outbreaks unless you catch them early. Bits of white fluff could be citrus mealybugs (Planococcus citri). Flat tan bumps are often brown soft scale (Coccus hesperidum). Faint speckling and webbing usually mean twospotted spider mites (Tetranychus urticae), which thrive in dry, heated rooms. Gently dab mealybug clusters with a cotton swab dipped in rubbing alcohol, or treat leaf undersides with a gentle organic insecticidal soap or neem oil wash. Bonide Captain Jack’s Neem Oil Spray, available from Amazon, is a brilliant organic way of controlling spider mites, scale, and mealybugs.

By giving your peace lily a little focused attention in November, you provide the support it needs to navigate the winter months with ease. Dialing back the watering can, finding a brighter pocket of filtered daylight, raising ambient moisture levels, and letting the roots rest will keep the foliage healthy and glossy.

Set up well in November, a peace lily usually comes through winter looking much like it did in summer, just growing a little slower. The fertilizer and the bigger pot can wait until spring, and so can you. When longer spring days return, your well-rested peace lily will be primed to surge back into active growth, rewarding you with fresh green leaves and a striking display of signature white blooms.

Coinbase Bank Gets Full OCC Approval as Ripple Leads Crypto Banking Push

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Ahmed Barakat is a journalist and copywriter based in Georgia with a growing focus on blockchain technology, DeFi, AI, privacy, digital assets, and fintech innovation.

The CryptoNews editorial team is composed of seasoned writers specializing in cryptocurrency and blockchain technology. Their expertise ensures comprehensive, accurate, and insightful content for...

Coinbase has moved closer to the center of the US financial system after securing full approval for its national trust bank charter. The Office of the Comptroller of the Currency (OCC) recorded the charter as effective on November 6. The development gives Coinbase a federally supervised framework for expanding its institutional custody and digital asset services.

The approval completes a process that began with Coinbase’s application in October 2025. The OCC granted conditional approval on April 2, 2026, before recording the charter as consummated. Coinbase can now operate through its national trust company under federal supervision.

Coinbase follows Ripple’s final OCC approval on October 26. Two major crypto companies have now secured national trust bank charters within weeks. Their progress shows how digital asset firms are increasingly seeking access to regulated financial infrastructure rather than operating outside traditional banking frameworks.

Discover: The Crypto Presales This Q4 2026

Coinbase’s approval follows a similar regulatory path taken by Ripple. The company received conditional approval for Ripple National Trust Bank in December 2025. Its final approval arrived on October 26, completing the process ahead of Coinbase.

Ripple’s charter has specific implications for its stablecoin business. Ripple National Trust Bank can manage the segregated reserves backing RLUSD. It can also act as collateral trustee and provide fiduciary digital asset custody services.

RLUSD will continue to be issued by Standard Custody & Trust Company, Ripple’s New York-regulated trust company. The new federal charter adds another layer of supervision around its reserve management and institutional services.

Coinbase Bank has a different commercial opportunity. Its national trust company can provide federally supervised custody and fiduciary services to institutional clients. Coinbase previously said the charter would support its Prime custody business and other regulated services.

The growing number of crypto trust bank approvals has not gone unchallenged. The Independent Community Bankers of America (ICBA) filed a lawsuit against the OCC on October 2. The group argues that the regulator exceeded its authority by expanding access to national trust charters.

The banking association claims crypto companies receive federal banking credibility without meeting several requirements imposed on traditional banks. These include FDIC insurance, certain capital and liquidity standards, and Community Reinvestment Act obligations.

🚨 BIG: ICBA Sues OCC Over Crypto Trust Bank ChartersThe Independent Community Bankers of America (ICBA) has sued the OCC, arguing that its rules allowing crypto firms to obtain national trust bank charters exceed the agency’s legal authority and do not impose the same… pic.twitter.com/kJ8fVcXPoj

The lawsuit challenges the OCC’s March 2026 rule and related guidance. It argues that the regulator has stretched the national trust charter beyond its original statutory purpose.

The dispute could have consequences beyond Coinbase and Ripple. A successful challenge could complicate the regulatory pathway for other digital asset companies pursuing similar charters.

However, the OCC’s approach reflects a wider shift in US financial regulation. The agency has increasingly opened the door to crypto custody, payments, and stablecoin-related activities under federal supervision. This shift also creates opportunities for emerging blockchain infrastructure projects.

Coinbase Bank’s approval represents more than another regulatory milestone. It shows that crypto companies are competing for a larger role in the financial infrastructure institutions will use.

Air Algerie VIP Lounge Algiers Airport Review: Strong Furniture Store Vibes

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

For the next stop of my Orient Express adventure via Africa trip, I had the chance to visit the Air Algerie VIP Lounge at Algiers Houari Boumediene International Airport (ALG). I had just landed from Montreal (YUL) in Air Algerie’s A330neo business class, and was connecting to Tunis (TUN) in Air Algerie’s A320 business class (a wet leased flight).

The Air Algerie VIP Lounge Algiers is actually pleasant enough. It’s quirky, as it has a ton of seating, and almost feels more like a furniture store than an airport lounge. But I appreciate that the space isn’t crowded, so it’s a nice enough place to pass some time. The food and drink selection is totally unremarkable and okay, so at least it’s not negatively memorable.

The Algiers Airport transit experience was interesting. Algeria is a country that really doesn’t want tourists (getting a visa is a pain), and Air Algerie doesn’t get much connecting international traffic, so not that many people are in international transit.

Upon landing from Montreal, I followed the signage in the direction of “Flight Connections.” Interestingly, I seemed to be among the only people on my flight with an international connection, as everyone else headed to immigration.

I ended up in the “transit room,” as it’s called. There my boarding pass was checked, and then I was given a form that I had to fill out. A form to transit internationally? Odd, but whatever. I then had to wait for an immigration officer to be ready, but he just seemed to be sitting in his window for several minutes without signaling he was ready, so I just waited.

After about five minutes, it was my turn. Then there was a security check, except the security guy clearly wasn’t quite ready yet for anyone, so he was caught off guard by me, and took a second to set things up.

All-in-all, it was a painless enough transit experience thanks to the lack of crowds, even if it was a bit bureaucratic. The Algiers Airport terminal is really big, and it just felt totally deserted during my early morning visit (when a lot of flights leave, in theory).

Once in the international departures area, it was easy to find the Air Algerie VIP Lounge, as there was signage for it right there. The lounge is in the center of the terminal, up a level, so you can either take the elevator, escalator, or stairs.

Once on the upper level, the entrance to the lounge was right there. I loved the Air Algerie Boeing 767 airplane model at the entrance. I’m not sure if this was intended to be a throwback, or if they just hadn’t bothered getting a new model in the nearly decade since the aircraft type was retired. I have a guess, but… 😉

The Air Algiers VIP Lounge Algiers is open 24/7, or something like that. The hours aren’t actually published, but the idea is that if there are flights, then the lounge should also be open.

The Air Algerie VIP Lounge is the primary lounge at Algiers Airport. So in addition to being open to premium passengers on Air Algerie, it also acts as the contract lounge for premium passengers on EgyptAir, Qatar Airways, Royal Jordanian, Saudia, Tunisair, and Turkish Airlines.

Oddly, the lounge isn’t open to Priority Pass members, despite clearly having the capacity to handle more passengers.

The Air Algerie VIP Lounge Algiers is quite a large facility, especially in relation to demand. The lounge basically consists of one large room, and it “opens up” into the rest of the terminal, as it’s essentially just on top of the main passenger concourse.

As I said above, the only way I can describe this lounge is as having very strong furniture store vibes, as there’s just a sea of couches and chairs, all of which are quite plush. I’ll let the pictures speak mostly for themselves.

The lounge was really quiet during my visit. There were maybe a total of a dozen people in the lounge, and I’d say fewer than half of those were passengers, and the remainder were airport staff having their morning coffee, and helping themselves to the buffet.

The lounge was quite well staffed — there were two staff members responsible for overseeing the buffet, while for reasons I can’t figure out, there were three people at the front desk of the lounge (I don’t think a single other person entered during my hour stay, so that strikes me as an overkill, but…).

The Air Algerie VIP Lounge Algiers has a self-serve food & drink selection.

When it comes to food, the selection included finger sandwiches, whole fruit, veggies, cheese, cold cuts, yogurt, cereal, pastries, etc.

There were also some hot options, none of which looked particularly appealing to me (and I also wondered for how long these items had been sitting around). This included eggs, sausage, potatoes, and veggies.

When it comes to drinks, this is a “dry” lounge (so there’s no alcohol). Options included coffee, tea, soft drinks, and water, all of which guests could help themselves to.

The Air Algerie VIP Lounge Algiers has bathrooms that are located just inside the entrance and to the right. They’re down a hall, right next to the kitchen area.

The men’s room had three sinks, three toilet stalls, and two urinals.

In theory there was also a shower suite, but as you can see below, not really.

The Air Algerie VIP Lounge Algiers was the best part of my Air Algerie experience, which isn’t saying a whole lot. The carrier’s flagship lounge is large, and has an okay selection of food and drinks. In an era where so many lounges are crowded, it’s at least nice to have a quiet space like this.

Admittedly this is otherwise a pretty uninteresting lounge, with few amenities, and a lack of variety when it comes to seating options. I definitely wouldn’t arrive early to use this lounge, but it’s also not a bad place to pass some time.

What do you make of the Air Algerie VIP Lounge Algiers?

Phantom Blade Zero Player Is Making Game’s Best Bosses Playable

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Sam Woods is TheGamer’s Senior Reporter. He’s been in games journalism since 2020 and has undertaken a variety of roles in that time. These have included roles such as SEO Editor, Managing Editor at DualShockers, and Global Head of Gaming, and he's also been seen on the BBC. He was promoted to the Senior Reporter position in 2025 following a series of high-quality reports and exclusives for TheGamer.

Since 2020, Sam has written over 3,000 articles and specializes in telling the stories of the people behind the games. He’s interviewed the biggest game directors, some of the industry’s top talents, told the stories of how indie games came to be, and spoken to players about what really makes them tick. He’s also interviewed some of the world’s biggest musical artists to find out how the medium has inspired their work, and TV and Film actors about their roles in video game adaptations. He even hosted a PAX panel in 2021 and made a documentary in 2024 surrounding the creation of a Disney game.

Alongside this, Sam focuses on breaking news. He got the scoop on Perfect Dark’s cancellation, provided context on some AI drama, and revealed that Lady Dimitrescu’s actress would love to step back into the character’s giant shoes. These articles have been picked up by the biggest gaming sites, including IGN, GameSpot, and PC Gamer. He also loves to dive into the numbers behind the games.

Sam’s journey in games journalism has taken him all over the world. He’s covered Gamescom in Germany, Summer Game Fest in Los Angeles, and Tokyo Game Show in Japan, each on three occasions.

He’s been learning Japanese since 2024 with the intention of identifying and translating Japanese-language news and bringing it to English-language audiences first, something he’s done countless times.

Sam’s first review was 2020’s Battletoads reboot, and he’s since reviewed games in The Legend of Zelda, Super Mario, and Pokemon franchises.

While Phantom Blade Zero is a truly excellent game (scoring a 5/5 in my official review), players have been hard at work doing what they can to improve it in the 10 days since it launched. We've seen full Soulslike transformations, game-changing cheat menus, and the addition of Sun Wukong from Chinese folklore.

It feels like the type of game that's going to get consistent support from its already ginormous player base, with that support continuing over recent times. One player is seemingly on a mission to make the game's best bosses into playable characters, with three already completed and ready to go.

Over on Nexus Mods, creator QOutlaw has been creating playable versions of some of Phantom Blade Zero's most memorable bosses. They've made three so far, and they're all excellent.

The first they released was a playable version of Bai Chilian, including her outfits and voice. It's received over 23,000 downloads so far.

Bai Chilian is arguably the best boss from Phantom Blade Zero's second area, Crimson Forest. She has two phases: the first is a more grounded fight where she summons ribbon snakes to aid her in battle. In the second, she becomes possessed, attacking from strings around the arena. If you collect all of the memory flowers related to her, you are assisted in battle by one of Soul's allies. It's a great fight, and while you'll be playing as her base form in this mod, equipping the White Serpent and Crimson Viper swords will make it a lot of fun.

The second mod QOutlaw uploaded is a playable Yuding- No.12 Crescent Blade Puppet. This fight happens late in Phantom Blade Zero and has some of the best animations in the entire game. She also blesses you with what I believe is the best weapon of the game's 30. Probably not as iconic as Bai Chilian, but a great option.

The final mod turns Soul into Mo Yuan, the masked man found in the Loong challenges, played by Donnie Yen. He's a pivotal character in Phantom Blade Zero's overarching story and is vital to the game's true ending. He's also exceptionally cool. And he's Donnie Yen.

I love the work that modders do, and installing any of these will make future NG+ runs of Phantom Blade Zero feel entirely different. A big recommendation from me.

The Midterms Were a Pretty Good Sign for the Right-to-Repair Movement

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Right-to-repair policies weren’t the headline issue in the midterms, but lawmakers across party lines ran on fixing the systems that lock buyers out.

Iowa senator-elect Josh Turek and presumptive Minnesota state representative Jake Johnson both centered repair policies in their platforms, focusing on issues that affect farmers and their equipment. (As of publication, Johnson’s race has not yet been called and may be headed to a recount.) In St. Paul, Minnesota, voters also passed a measure ensuring renters' right to repair with overwhelming support.

The right-to-repair movement, propelled by advocates in the US and internationally, pushes lawmakers for rules that prevent manufacturers from locking owners out of their own devices. Repair efforts tend to be bipartisan, with support ranging from consumers to farmers to wheelchair users.

“A political climate where people want policies that stand up for the little guy, help reduce unnecessary costs, and work in a bipartisan way—that’s a very good environment for right-to-repair,” says Nathan Proctor, senior director of PIRG’s right-to-repair campaign, in a text to WIRED.

Advancing right-to-repair legislation would be a step toward the larger ownership battles taking place in the US. People are angry that companies are turning everything into a subscription service or using software locks to restrict people from modifying or fixing their gear. That meshes with a long-simmering fury about the broader political climate, where tariffs, a memory shortage, and an extremely unpopular war with Iran have driven prices up on just about everything.

As Kevin O’Reilly, executive director of Fulu, an advocacy group focused on consumer digital ownership and product rights, points out, the efforts to claw back control over devices mimic the broader message voters sent at the ballot box.

“There’s a lot to be excited about,” O’Reilly tells WIRED. “People want things to be more affordable, politics to be less polarized, and Congress to solve problems that affect them.”

That discontent points straight to the root of the problem: Section 1201 of the Digital Millennium Copyright Act (DMCA). The 1998 federal statute made it illegal to circumvent digital locks, enabling companies to punish people who break them with possible prison time. Repair advocates have widely decried the legislation, as companies have used it to prevent people from fixing their devices without software access.

For advocates, the reshuffled Congress is the cleanest legislative target in decades. With voter frustration cutting cleanly across party lines, O'Reilly suggests the mandate is hard to ignore.

“If I were a new or returning member of Congress,” O’Reilly says, “I would see DMCA reform as a no-brainer.”
